- mr president, since my election to the european parliament in 1994, i have never read a report with so much anti-irish sentiment and overtones as the jonckheer report we are debating today. i would quote the report: 'state aid per capita is highest in italy, germany and ireland, the latter country being clearly in the lead when national aid and regional and social aid are added together'. i think the rapporteur is simply playing with numbers. i am having a hard time understanding how he can introduce social and regional funds into this mathematical equation. i would remind him that the european union has approved the new guidelines on regional aid for the period after the year 2000, which was just an extension of the policy objectives to complete the internal market within europe. regional disparities must be overcome if the internal market is to be successful and prosperous. i welcome the fact that 40% aid and an additional 15% aid for smes for fixed income investments will be authorised for companies setting up in objective i regions in europe after the year 2000. i would remind mr jonckheer that irish companies or foreign companies based in ireland still have to cross two seas to reach the european market on the continent. no other member state is at such a disadvantage.
- mr president, i should like to concentrate on the question of the corpus juris. i would like to support mrs morgan's comments on this matter wholeheartedly. the corpus juris was drawn up in the absence of any public debate or participation. the idea of a european prosecutor in the image of the continental inquisitorial model, with predominant jurisdiction over the whole territory of the eu, would have a major impact on the traditional systems of ireland and the united kingdom. member states have the right to receive information on the notion of a single criminal code and a european prosecutor. indeed, when the project was originally drawn up, it was supposed to be limited to cases of fraud to the detriment of the eu budget. however, in 1977, when the corpus juris was actually launched in san sebastian - in front of a very closed audience of 140 lawyers, excluding the media - the then president of the european parliament, mr gil-robles gil-delgado, said that he considered it a draft and that the intention was to extend the eu's competence in criminal matters to all areas of criminal activity. we must start a public debate on this issue. member states and citizens within member states must be properly informed. the threat to the traditional legal systems in ireland and the united kingdom needs to be addressed. we need much more openness and transparency on this issue than has been the case so far. it is unacceptable that such a project has been imposed on the eu member states without adequate public debate.
in writing. - the schroedter report talks about the need to promote partnerships in view of the eu structural funds' spending in europe for the period 2000-2006. i think this is particularly important given that eu structural funds are always spent in a way that maximises the economic development of various regions when local and regional authorities are involved in the decision-making process regarding the spending of such funds. as a member of the european parliament for leinster, i have always supported the need to implement projects with a pyramid scheme and supported by national eu funds. i believe that the irish government, the european commission and various eu governments cannot decide on specific spending priorities alone. i believe that local authorities and groups from the private and voluntary sectors should be fully involved in the decision-making process on the spending of european structural funds. we have seen, for example, the success of the leader i and leader ii programmes in ireland in terms of the jobs created through the administration of these programmes in rural areas in ireland and in europe. the leader iii programme is to be implemented later this year. the leader programme, at its core, offers an opportunity for public, private and voluntary groups to pool their common talents in order to create long-term and sustainable jobs in small and medium-sized enterprises in rural areas. this classic example illustrates how the partnership concept operates and such programmes should be supported to the fullest extent of its powers. the european peace and reconciliation fund has also been successful in creating jobs in the border counties of ireland. once again, the private, public and voluntary sectors are actively involved, illustrating how private funds can best be spent to help the various local job creation projects in this region. in the next period of application of the eu structural funds - 2000-2006 - other eu initiatives, such as equal and urban, will be applied. i believe, once again, that these initiatives must include the involvement of local authorities and communities in order to identify the areas where european structural funds would benefit the most. the rapporteur refers to the need to implement an integrated approach for the new plans and programmes of the eu structural funds. they must promote a decentralised, effective and comprehensive partnership based on the skills and commitment of all sectors within regional and local authorities. this is a very delicate issue because the major economic and social difficulties can only be overcome by a crucial integration between national, european and local funding.

the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: the protection of the environment, the protection of the environment against pollution, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of chemicals, and the protection of the environment. i have to say that i am not satisfied with the way in which the commission has been able to achieve this. i'm glad we were able to write it so quickly. it represents three to four months of intensive work since the new commission was appointed in september last year. it includes the extensive consultations carried out in recent years since the publication of the commission's green paper on food law. it also reflects our recent food-warning experiences in areas such as dioxin and sewage sludge, and the bse crisis. the white paper also reflects the concerns of this parliament which you have expressed to both mr prodi and myself on many occasions when we have debated food security in this house since the commission was appointed. i need not remind you that consumer confidence in europe's food safety regime has been severely affected by the crises and alarms of recent years and months. the commission is fully committed to restoring this trust by putting in place the most modern and effective food safety regime in the world. when i launched the white paper last week, i said that the cartridge was one of the most powerful weapons in the world. the most informed decisions are made by european consumers. if their trust is compromised, this is reflected in purchasing decisions, with dramatic effects on farmers, producers and the industry in general. in an industry that weighs some 600 billion euros a year, even the slightest drop in confidence can have significant effects. from the agri-food sector to the livestock sector, the number of jobs is 10 million. high levels of confidence are needed to increase employment and improve competitiveness. this crisis of confidence has also had an unfortunate and unavoidable effect: it has eroded consumer confidence in national and european systems and institutions which should monitor and guarantee the highest standards of food safety. that said, i would like to make it clear that europe nonetheless has one of the best food industries in the world, and one of the safest food control systems. the challenge is to make it the cream of the crop. the overall objective of the white paper on food safety is therefore to put in place the necessary legislation and structures to ensure the highest possible standards of consumer health protection in relation to food consumption. we are setting out a timetable for challenging and ambitious change. the commission will need the unconditional support of parliament if it is to achieve its ambitions in time. we will also need the broadest possible support from the council and other key players. the white paper on food safety outlines a full range of actions needed to complement and modernise current eu food legislation. all these measures are aimed at making it more coherent, comprehensible and flexible. we want to promote better implementation of this legislation and provide more transparency to consumers. the detailed action plan on food security contained in the white paper sets out a precise timetable for action over the next three years. more than 80 measures are envisaged. our aim is to have a coherent and modern body for food law by the end of 2002. we are also considering the establishment of a european food authority by 2002 as an essential complement to the new food safety regime. this idea will be the subject of much examination and debate. it has already generated a lot of comments, including reactions from members of parliament. the chapter in the white paper on the establishment of a european food authority is clearly intended to generate opinions and comments. we shall be seeking to know the views of the public on our ideas by the end of april. i shall return to this consultation process in a moment. the commission considers that major structural changes are needed in our food safety system to ensure that the dual objective of ensuring the highest standards of food safety and restoring consumer confidence is achieved. why should a european food authority be an essential part of this change structure? the first major concern is that of independence. key players, including consumers, want an independent system and to be regarded as independent by all parties directly concerned. excellence and transparency should also be ensured. we have made a lot of progress in recent years since the reformed scientific advice system was adopted in the wake of the bse crisis. however, the commission believes that we must go further. we need to create a permanent, truly independent, high-quality and transparent risk assessment system. the central task of this authority will be risk assessment in the field of food safety. we are considering transferring the work of the five existing scientific committees on food safety to this authority. these may not be transferred in their present form or structure - we will wait for opinions before we draw up our final proposals on the establishment of the european food authority. however, if we were to propose a simple transfer, it is clear that this would not be sufficient. as the white paper makes clear, the new authority must be a value-added structure. however, i believe that the current system of providing scientific advice needs to be strengthened. i am thinking of stronger scientific and other support within the authority for independent scientists. i also envisage the authority taking much more initiative than the current regime - anticipating rather than reacting, identifying problems before they become crises. this proactive approach should become the hallmark of this authority. in order to adopt a proactive approach, the white paper identifies a number of new areas which would be covered by this authority. these include comprehensive information gathering and monitoring functions, coordination of scientific information within the eu and the establishment of strong networks with food safety agencies and bodies within the member states. we also envisage that one of the tasks of this authority will be to manage an improved early warning system for matters affecting food and feed. the commission decided that it was neither appropriate nor feasible to delegate risk management powers to the authority. we believe that decisions in the field of risk management should remain the sole prerogative of the commission, parliament and the council. i have no qualms about defending this approach which i firmly believe is the right one. of course, some would argue that we should give legislative powers to such an authority. i do not accept this view and i reject it with some force. the amendment of the treaty to give parliament a much greater role in the legislative process was only introduced last year. to assign such a role to an authority at this stage would, in my view, be a step backwards and a dilution of democratic accountability. i would be very interested to hear the views of parliament itself on this matter. others claim that the commission may indeed ignore the opinion of the new authority. i reject that argument as well. how could a commissioner for health and consumer protection reject or ignore independent and well-founded scientific advice on food safety? would that be in the interests of the citizens of europe? in my view, that is not the case at all, unless the rejection of scientific arguments is based on sound grounds, rational reasoning and is fully justified. it seems difficult to envisage such a situation evolving. i can assure you today that the commission, in the exercise of its risk management functions, will take full account of the scientific advice of this authority. i have already said that this authority will be responsible for developing networks with national food safety agencies and bodies in the member states. this is a crucial task. we must develop greater certainty about the science that underpins food security in the european union. the authority should become the authoritative source of scientific advice and information on food safety issues. this state of affairs will not be established by the mere creation of this authority but will be established over time in parallel with the confidence that the authority itself will acquire. i don't think we can be normative in the areas of science and science-based opinions. however, through the evolution of dynamic networks through national scientific bodies and agencies, this authority will become prominent at european level. i am also anxious to hear parliament's views on this matter. as part of a value-added structure, the white paper proposes that the authority should play a major role in risk communication: disseminating complex scientific information in a way that is convenient for consumers; providing the obvious and mandatory gateway to the most up-to-date risk data; be clearly visible; be a good news announcer in food matters; be dynamic. the white paper also contains very important proposals concerning controls. this is a crucial component of the system of checks and countermeasures to ensure that member states and operators comply with community legislation. i want to see a real internal market in the field of controls. in this context, we also propose to significantly strengthen the control function of the food and veterinary office in dublin. this revised community framework would have three central elements: operational criteria established at community level, community control guidelines, and improved administrative cooperation in the development and management of controls. in the context of our proposals in this area - which i hope to put forward later this year - i will consider whether additional powers should be granted to the commission in addition to infringement procedures. these could include a deduction from community financial aid or the recovery of funds already committed to a member state. these proposals must be seen as part of our mission to establish the highest food standards in the world, to increase consumer confidence and to expand markets for farmers and producers within the union. in addition to proposals for the establishment of a new european food authority and an improved system of controls at community level, the white paper proposes an action plan with a wide range of measures to improve and bring coherence to community legislation covering all aspects of food production from the stable to the table. it sets out more than 80 specific actions for the coming period and aims to fill the gaps identified in the current legislation. the new legal framework will cover animal feed, animal health and welfare, hygiene, contaminants and residues, novel foods, additives, flavourings, packaging and irradiation. it will include a proposal on general food law which will contain food safety principles such as the responsibility of feed manufacturers, farmers and those working in the food sector, the traceability of feed, food and ingredients, adequate risk analysis through, for example, risk assessment - namely scientific advice and analysis of information - risk management - namely regulation and control - risk communication and the application of the precautionary principle where appropriate. as regards the precautionary principle, i would add that the commission is currently finalising a communication which i hope will be adopted very soon. i look forward to an exchange of views this afternoon with members of parliament who would clearly have preferred this discussion to take place last week if an appropriate parliamentary forum had been available. however, in view of my contacts with the chairmen of the relevant committees, i admit that this was not feasible. but i also know that we will have many opportunities in the coming months to look at the white paper proposals on this authority. we now have a few months to hold the necessary debate on the commission's ideas for the white paper on the establishment of a european food authority. parliament will play a key role in this debate. parliament has played a crucial role in europe's response to the bse crisis. since then, he has been particularly active in highlighting citizens' concerns about food security issues. i expect parliament to make a similarly strong and constructive contribution to the debate on this authority. we will have a few months until the end of april to debate this issue and to gather our views, although i fully recognise that this is also a very tight timetable. i would therefore ask parliament to make the appropriate arrangements to ensure that these views can be expressed as quickly as possible. it is essential that the commission has parliament's input in creating what would be a key element in enhancing the level of consumer health protection and thus restoring consumer confidence in the european union's food safety regime. the european food authority will be a crucial ingredient in the eu food safety regime for years to come. it's essential that our work be successful.

knowledge. we intend to follow up this council with an action plan: e-europe for the information society. the european union has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the field of defence. minister gama was right to stress the need to bridge the gap between europe's economic strength and its political influence, not in the name of some post-imperial vanity, but because that is what our citizens and the world expect of us. the presidency will see to the establishment of the interim political and security committee. i have proposed that the commission should set up a to make funds available rapidly for crisis prevention and management activities, as appropriate. this should include, for example, the rapid deployment of police forces or teams to monitor human have eaten a lot and the walls that i have climbed a lot because of my frustration with the length of time it takes to get things done. this is intolerable. that has to change and it will change. middle east. i know that if an agreement is reached between syria and israel, the israeli prime minister will still have to count, in any future referendum, on a real opposition to any new agreement israeli-syrian question would look promising. as far as palestine is concerned, i recognize that there are still difficulties in implementing certain aspects of the wye plantation agreements. at the suspend the use of building permits already granted and not to grant any new ones. clearly these problems are responsible for the stagnation of progress in the final status negotiations, although i do not think that these negotiations will remain at a standstill indefinitely. the main difficulty at the moment in the negotiations seems to be that the palestinians insist that the problem of the borders be settled first, whereas the israeli government believes that the problem can only be settled if a solution is found first on the settlements and security. at the moment, both sides seem to agree that nothing will be settled until everything is settled. in conclusion, while it is true that the other countries of the middle east had expressed reservations about the general direction of the peace process, now that the syrian government has joined the process, egypt and other countries in the region are also showing a willingness to move forward. i sincerely hope that, in the interest of improving the security and quality of life of the people of this region, the main actors will make rapid progress in the very near future towards a comprehensive agreement on all the major issues that need to be resolved.

- (en) to put this debate in context, i would point out that the current pop, the fourth in the name, is far from following the scientific recommendations of the lassen commission. the commission recommended a massive reduction in the eu fleet to allow the recovery of fish stocks. the council was not able to accept them, so the reductions requested in its final regulations are relatively modest. thus, at the end of the five years of the programme, the combined eu fleets are assumed to have reduced tonnage by 2.3% and engine power by 3%. as the commission report shows, at the beginning of 1998, just one year after the programme started, the combined eu fleets were already far from meeting these objectives. this result is not evidence of the goodwill of the member states; it shows that the reductions requested were indeed very modest and far from sufficient. however, and this is the real stumbling block, many member states have not complied with the legislation and many fleets are too large. what should be done with those member states which place themselves above community legislation and ignore their obligations? the commission has made several proposals: it can suspend the allocation of the structural funds, but this instrument is only valid for those member states which actually use these funds. it can bring an action against the member states concerned, but this is a long, costly and difficult solution. in its proposal for the recent revision of the ifp, the commission suggested denying infringing states access to fishing licences granted under fisheries agreements with third countries. as you might expect, the council refused. effective means must be found to persuade or oblige member states to respect their legal obligations. mr cunha asked the commission to consider the possibility of applying sanctions at the quota level in the event of non-compliance with the objectives of the pop. some consider it to be a breach of the principle of relative stability. but let us not forget that this principle of relative stability is only one of many. the basic rules of the common fisheries policy also stipulate that community management systems must achieve a permanent balance between available resources and fishing effort. in other words, if all stocks disappear, the relative stability of a zero quota will no longer make much sense. we think that this idea deserves at least some serious consideration. there is already a precedent in regulation 847/96 on multiannual tacs. under this regulation, a member state that exceeds its quota in certain fisheries is subject to a reduction in its quota the following year, not only by the proportion of overfishing but also by an additional amount proportional to the overfishing recorded. it is clear that this is a fine, but it was not considered to be a breach of the principle of relative stability. perhaps mr cunha could have formulated his request differently, so as to make it clear that, if there is a reduction, it would not be permanent but limited in time. that is why we support the amendment by the socialist group to insert the term 'temporary'. i think everyone will agree that we need to find a way to enforce the law in the member states.
- mr president, i would like to say that i fully support the report drawn up by mr cunha. i have some reservations, but i see no other way of getting things done. it is interesting to note that the member states with the largest fishing fleets today built them up in the 16th and 17th centuries. in ireland we are suffering terribly from the quotas imposed on us and the capacity of our fleet. let me give you a historical overview: at the time when the other maritime nations - britain, spain and holland - were building their fleets in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries, there was a law against irish boats transporting fish. that is why we still have the same type of boat today, the little curragh, on which st brendan sailed to america in the 18th century. that is why the fishermen i represent are suffering so much today. today the netherlands has seven times as many boats over 24 metres as ireland, even though our fisheries resources are far greater. i would like to express our dissatisfaction and say that if we exploited the oceans as we should and if we conserved and managed our fisheries as we should, we could probably catch twice as many fish in european waters as we do now. we would not create jobs, since we already have sufficient capacity, but we could more than double the profits compared to today. by establishing a reasonable conservation system and imposing restrictions today, we will ensure resources for future generations. when we have done that, and despite the sanctity of the principle of relative stability, i sincerely hope that a country like ireland, with its atlantic frontage and vast resources, will one day be able to enjoy a more equitable sharing.

- mr president, i welcome this report and thank mrs ramos for her detailed and constructive introduction. it is useful to make this effort in order to promote a better dialogue between the european union and the fisheries sector. in my own region of the european union, which is a peripheral region, we have always had great difficulty in getting the idea of a common fisheries policy accepted by fishermen. since mr maccormick has raised the matter - and i have already referred to it earlier - the position of ireland is difficult because if you take ireland out of the european union, you will be taking a lot of fish out of the sea. traditionally, irish fishermen did not catch this fish because, as some people know, at one time in our history it was against the law to transport fish in irish vessels. this situation continued until the beginning of this century. so people were still fishing with old curraghs 20 years before we joined the european union. in any case, it is a fact that fish from irish waters is worth about 700 million euros, and perhaps more since the price of fish has changed. as a result of the cuts in the structural funds allocated to ireland, due to its economic growth, the value of fish caught in waters considered to be irish if the european union did not exist will, in a few years, exceed the total of the structural funds allocated to ireland. this proposal is therefore even more difficult to win. i do not know whether the same is true in other countries of the european union. scotland, which is part of the united kingdom, withdraws its natural share with the united kingdom, but ireland is the only country that does not. it is the only natural resource that all member states recognise as the common property of the european union. this does not mean that we should not pursue this dialogue. the extension of the 20 mile limit agreed by parliament may be part of the solution and may help to satisfy local fishermen by giving them more control over their lives. i am not seeking renationalisation, but regionalisation would certainly help to solve the problems facing scotland and ireland.

- i welcome commissioner barnier and the portuguese presidency to this house this afternoon. the igc will be launched on 14 february and will be completed by the end of 2000. the task is enormous but it can be accomplished. i think that there is a general agreement among all the groups here this afternoon that the helsinki agenda will not be sufficient to cover the reform needed to prepare europe for enlargement. in other words, we must go beyond the important "relics of amsterdam". these include the extension of qualified majority voting - we accept this in my country, except in taxation matters - the re-weighting of voting in the council to favour the largest states, and the number of commissioners in an enlarged europe. on the last point, ireland wants the right to appoint a full and non-discriminatory member of the commission to be maintained, regardless of the number of member states joining europe. we are prepared to consider a re-weighting of votes in the council of ministers, provided that the largest member states agree that each member state has a full and non-discriminatory member in the commission. when i raise this particular point, i know that i am speaking on behalf of a great many smaller states. we should also discuss at the igc the possible division of the treaties into two parts - a political and a constitutional area. we are prepared to accept this sharing if it does not limit the control that the smaller member states had in the renegotiation of all the policies of the treaty. in other words, if we are not fully represented in the commission, we will not contribute to the political discussions. so we are following this whole thing very closely. we welcome the idea of a charter of fundamental rights and look forward to seeing what it contains. mr president, the treaty of amsterdam set the number of members of the european parliament at 700 and we must discuss in parliament how these 700 seats will be distributed in an enlarged europe.

- mr president, the european union has economic frontiers, and the internal market, with the free movement of goods, people, services and capital, is now fully realized. but for the internal market to function effectively and for the single european currency to be a success, it is important that all of europe's more than 100 regions are able to compete economically in this challenging environment. some regions of the european union are very strong economically and have a level of income well above the average per capita. the union has poor regions which need european aid to improve their economic fabric and to be competitive in the european union structure. this is why, when we look at the financial perspectives of the eu between 1989 and 1993, and 1994 and 1999, we see that a large part of the eu budget was earmarked for the administration of the european regional development fund and the european social fund. many regions of europe still face two problems: firstly, the lack of adequate infrastructure in terms of roads, water treatment facilities and related transport networks. secondly, the need to develop initiatives to combat youth unemployment and longterm unemployment, a persistent social problem in many urban and rural areas of europe. we must always be committed to ensuring that we do not limit ourselves to building a europe of cities. we must ensure that job creation initiatives are put in place to promote employment opportunities in the small and mediumsized enterprise sector also in rural areas of europe. the main elements of this evening's debate concern the implementation of the eu initiative programmes, including the new interreg iii initiative, equal and the leader plus programme. with these three programmes we must have clear evidence of the eu's commitment to promoting crossborder development, tackling the problems of longterm unemployment and supporting rural development programmes.
- mr president, i want to contribute to this debate because the urban initiative has been productive, in ireland in particular, and i would very much like the european union to make a further contribution in this respect. it is sad that several hundred, if not thousands, of communities in the european union suffer from severe poverty and are severely disadvantaged. even in very rich member states and cities, large numbers of people live in ghettos with inadequate facilities, where schooling is very poor, where physical infrastructure is underdeveloped and where drugs and other phenomena are prevalent. it seems to me that in order for the european union to prove that it is helping to help the citizens of the european union, it must help the member states to demonstrate that the union is working for those citizens and their families. this programme has been very productive in ireland, as i know it has been in other countries. it took a while to get started there, but the reason for that was that the people in the region needed to develop these programs themselves. it is important that they use their own initiative and local skills in developing this initiative. it would be very easy to ensure rapid and timely development if outside professional experts were involved, but this would undermine the overall objective of the urban programme. i would like to make one further remark before concluding: we must insist that where funding is allocated and where this programme is developed, this is done within the framework of a serious urban development policy. unfortunately, this is not the case in ireland.

- mr president of the commission, may i say on behalf of my group that we largely support the starting point, the main points of analysis and the strategy which you have set out to this house today: namely that the issues at stake are, at present, unquestionably political in nature. they're political in nature because we're moving forward. although we have not yet completed the construction of all the economic building blocks, we are moving from this type of functional construction of europe to a much more challenging political perspective. the challenge of enlargement is political. the challenge of creating a domain dedicated to justice and home affairs, to dealing with asylum and immigration issues and crime is decidedly political. the challenge of a common foreign, security and defence policy is a decidedly political one. and it is because these challenges are political that they are becoming more complex. and it is because they are political that they are more sensitive subjects at the level of sovereignty. and it is because they are more sensitive to sovereignty that they are more sensitive to voters and citizens. that is why we must find a way to get closer to these political objectives, to attack not just the skepticism that surrounds us, but the most terrible enemy, namely the apathy that you have denounced. the means to achieve this are difficult but real. i welcome your view that we need greater synergy between our institutions, not just between the commission and parliament, but also between these two institutions and the council, in order to highlight the scale of this political challenge. we need more dialogue, not just with each other but also with civil society. we need further clarification on what europe wants to become. we must be able to face fears and anxieties, because in many ways the greatest fear lies in fear itself. perhaps, through more dialogue and clarification, we can overcome some of these problems. my group is particularly interested in the creative possibilities you have mentioned for the prospect of a white paper on governance next spring. in view of the interconnectedness of europe's networks that you describe, it is very important that europe focuses on its own essential activities and that we have the courage to suggest areas in which europe could possibly take a step back. this could convince citizens that europe, at the level of its governance, is not exclusively run from brussels, concentrating and grabbing ever more powers, more decisions, more authority. this is the political essence of the challenge which i think you have set out very clearly in this house today. for my group, enlargement is the first priority which should serve as a reference for all other reform possibilities, whether governance, the intergovernmental conference, institutional reform. we see enlargement as a partnership process and not as a process of dissociation between 'them and us'. we see enlargement as a positive combination of elements beneficial to the member states and the candidate countries. i suggest that the commission, as the repository of a huge amount of detailed information on enlargement, should present a document setting out the usefulness of enlargement, not just in budgetary and financial terms, but in a broader perspective; the european parliament has also been asked to present a document similar to the cecchini report, which preceded the single act, explaining to people, in a way that encourages them to engage in the debate, that this is a major, historic challenge facing europe today. we must be able to make thorough preparations in this field. as for south-east europe, i share your view that we must judge political institutions by their achievements. in the course of the term of office of this commission and this parliament, we have to carry out this difficult and not easy task of restoring peace in south-eastern europe. we strongly support the stabilisation and association agreements, but we would like to see this go further. we will publish this information and present it during the debate on the swoboda report later this week. this strategy paper highlights the global role of the european union. our main objective must be to give the union a weight in world affairs equivalent to its weight in aid, trade and finance. at the beginning of this decade we must look at how our institutions will develop internationally until the end of this decade, and in particular what place we should occupy in the international monetary bodies, what place we should occupy in the united nations bodies and so on. we must open this debate. in conclusion, this morning, the day after valentine's day, the newspapers are announcing that your honeymoon is over. i don't know if you're a romantic or not, mr. prodi. perhaps that's a very unromantic thing to say on a day like this. however, we in the liberal group welcome it because we feel that it means that we have now started work in earnest. it's the way of things. in view of the many adjectives you used in your speech to describe europe - an energetic europe, an enterprising europe, a europe with a human face and an inclusive europe - let me define, in a word dear to the hearts of my liberal colleagues, in two words, the europe we want to see in your governance plan: a europe of the possible. - madam president, the reform and democratisation of the institutions of the european union are at the heart of the historical and moral challenge of the enlargement process. this was the opening statement by commission president prodi when he presented the european commission's strategic objectives for the next five years. we are awaiting the white paper on european union governance, due to be published this summer, which will deal with the problem of balance between the governments of the member states and the institutions of the european union. on this specific point, i think it is important that we address the reform of the commission's internal decision-making procedures. the commission has indicated in its proposals for the forthcoming intergovernmental conference that it would prefer to see smaller member states lose their automatic right to appoint a member of the european commission. this is a scenario where the european union has more than 25 member states. i do not want a two-storey european union to be built. i believe that this would be contrary to the spirit and purpose of the treaty of rome and all subsequent treaties. there must be equality in terms of national representation within the commission as well as within all the other european institutions. i would remind those who seek to remove the right of small member states to appoint a european commissioner that the united states of america recognizes smaller states in the same way as larger states in the united states senate. virtually every one of the 50 states in the united states has two elected senators, regardless of population. any future reform of the european union will require a referendum in my country. it will be extremely difficult for those proposing the ratification of any future treaty to win the support of the irish people if we lose our right to have a representative in the european commission. the reform of the european council will undoubtedly also be included in the white paper on the governance of the european union to be published this summer. again, there are specific policy areas which should be left to the member states. i do not believe that there is broad support in europe for the introduction of qualified majority voting in the fields of taxation, justice and home affairs or foreign affairs at the level of the european union. currently, under article 99 of the treaty of rome, decisions at eu level on tax changes must be taken unanimously. i believe that this proposal should be retained, as a common european tax code would lead to a deterioration rather than an improvement in the smooth functioning of the european union. i am in favour of enlargement of the european union. i am in favour of institutional changes to ensure that this enlargement of the union is carried out in a rational and structured way. however, we must bear in mind that public opinion, with 370 million citizens in the european union, is an essential element to be taken into account when amending the eu treaties. the changes must not be too rapid and cannot be too radical, otherwise public opinion will make ratification of any future treaty on european union extremely difficult.
- madam president, the commission has been right to make food safety one of its top priorities. the recent dioxin scare in belgium, and before that the bse crisis in britain, and the ongoing controversy over the safety of genetically modified food have all contributed to undermining consumer confidence in the safety of the food they eat. if the commission is truly able to restore confidence in the food chain, it will at the same time restore confidence in the eu institutions and demonstrate their ability to protect the rights of eu citizens. i am therefore pleased that the issues of food safety, public health and consumer confidence are high on the agenda for the commission for the year 2000. however, i was disappointed by the nature of the proposal in the recent white paper to establish a european food authority. in its present form, what is being proposed can more easily be considered an advisory body to the commission than an independent body with similar decision-making and legislative powers to the us federal food and drug administration which has already demonstrated its credibility in this specific area. in addition, any future legislation will need to address the modus operandi of the relationship between the european food authority and national authorities in the member states, for example the food safety authority in ireland. this body, for example, is already doing good work alongside the recently established crossborder food safety body in ireland. it would be tragic if their efforts were undermined by the shortcomings of eu legislation. my fear is that these shortcomings in eu legislation could result in conflicts of competence between national and eu bodies, which should work together rather than against each other. this is a problem we must guard against.

- mr president, let me first say that i am entirely in favour of a statute for assistants. yesterday, when i arrived at parliament, i was handed a document concerning a demonstration proposed by the people present, to be held outside parliament. as all colleagues know - and many of you have spoken to me in the last six months - all such demonstrations and actions are first examined by the comart parliamentary committee. to give a non-controversial example, i would point out that no commercial events can be held in parliament for obvious reasons. following a letter i received yesterday, i immediately informed the attendees that we would propose that they be allowed to organize a meeting or demonstration in the probably near future, perhaps even next month. however, all demonstrations must take place in such a way as not to disturb the proper functioning of parliament. i signed a letter yesterday afternoon, during the bureau meeting, informing the assistants of these aspects. however, i was told that they had also been informed verbally of this decision by the committee of group chairmen last thursday, so that they were fully aware of the decision. there's nothing wrong with them telling us how they feel. the possibility of a meeting during the next parliamentary session is not inconvenient. however, there was a misunderstanding, because they received a letter from someone else, giving them the feeling that they had the authority. but all demonstrations are first examined by the comart commission. as you can see, there are many events taking place in the vicinity of the parliament, whether they concern different countries or have different objectives. this is not an attempt to censor or to violate the very real and legitimate principle that we should have a status for attendees.
- mr president, i would first like to congratulate the rapporteur, mr graefe zu baringdorf, on his report. it reflects the views and concerns of all eu citizens in the field of food safety and quality. the events of the past two years have certainly raised awareness and raised fears about the real threat to food security and public health. the promptness with which this parliament acted must be regarded by all citizens as the best guarantee for the future, because you have drawn up a programme on food quality which the member states must comply with. however, even in this crucial area to be managed, parliament is showing its commitment to respect the principle of subsidiarity by encouraging member states to assume and meet their responsibilities. in agenda 2000, i believe we have given priority to policies that directly affect citizens: food security, water quality, environmental protection and rural development. if we implement this programme with commitment and diligence, the beginning of this new millennium will be an important moment in the implementation of policies that are people-centred and largely reflective of the needs of the community. i particularly welcome the new and strict authorisation procedures which have been proposed for additives in animal feed. those who violate it will be dealt with severely. i would like to congratulate the new commissioner responsible for food safety, mr byrne. it is a difficult task, but it has responded quickly and effectively to the demands of this house and to the concerns of consumers. i am particularly pleased that my own country, ireland, is at the forefront of implementing the new food regulations which are based on the principle of traceability. this will allow ireland to be considered in the future as the centre of excellence in the field of food production.
- mr president, i congratulate the rapporteur on this excellent report. as an irish member of the european parliament from a country with a large supply of good quality water, i support the vast majority of the proposals in this directive. however, i should now like to examine the areas of disagreement between the environment committee of our parliament and the council. the council has already presented its common position on this subject. the council believes that the objective of satisfactory surface water status should be achieved within 16 years of the entry into force of this directive, whereas the parliament's committee on the environment would like to see this time limit reduced to 10 years. i see no reason why the member states of the european union should not be able to implement the essential provisions of this directive as quickly as possible. i now turn to the amendments which will be tabled in parliament tomorrow on the principle of recovery of water use costs. in its common position, the council states that the governments of the european union must take account of the principle of the recovery of water use costs. the common position does not, however, contain any specific date for the implementation of this principle. amendment no 43 aims to ensure that by 2010 water pricing policies in europe have adequate incentives for efficient water use. in addition, an appropriate contribution from different economic sectors, broken down by household, industrial and agricultural sectors, must guarantee the implementation of this policy. unless these amendments are supported in the vote tomorrow, it will send a strong message that water metering and taxation should be implemented for households in all the states of the european union. from an irish point of view, this is politically unfeasible, as it would also be from the point of view of other eu member states such as portugal, greece and spain.
commission - (sv) the commission is aware of the construction of this port, but it has no knowledge of the construction of this warehouse or of the other infrastructures mentioned by the honourable member. the construction of the port is being financed by the structural funds and has as its main result the dredging of the mouth of the boyne river and the temporary storage of sediment masses collected on the stegrennan mud-leash. the area around the boyne estuary has been designated as a special protection area under the wild birds directive. this means that any exploitation in or near the estuary must comply with the protection rules laid down for this region in the community's natural habitats directive. since 1998, the commission has received a number of complaints alleging that the construction of the port would result in damage to the stegrennan vase leash, which is now part of the protected area, and that the applicable protection rules had not been complied with. after examining these complaints during 1998 and early 1999 - a period during which the project did not receive any structural fund assistance - the commission became convinced in the summer of 1999 that the protection rules had been taken into account in the construction of the port. a detailed study of the environmental consequences had been carried out. the most negative consequence of the project, the loss of the stegrennan potting leash, is only temporary, as the contractor responsible for the work is obliged to rehabilitate the site. at the commission's request, the stegrennan mud flats were formally included in the boyne estuary special protection area, of which they were not previously a part. in addition, to compensate for the negative effects of this temporary disappearance of the mud-leash on the bird populations concerned, it was planned to improve other sites of introduction of these species in the estuary area. however, since the summer of 1999, this last measure has been the subject of a dispute. the irish authorities first reversed the improvement measure initially envisaged. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire. however, the new provision contained minor changes which led to a new complaint. given that ireland has committed itself to compensatory measures, and that a number of other problems have now been resolved, the commission cannot now propose to freeze financial assistance from the structural funds. nevertheless, it is prepared to discuss with the irish authorities the question of compensatory measures to improve certain natural habitats, particularly in the light of the legal action still pending before an irish court.
- i don't understand what the commissioner said. at this stage the stregrennan mud-rope, which was added to the special protection zone at the commission's request, has been completely destroyed. it is clear that the activities that took place there are in flagrant breach of european directives. you say there will be no further funding. i'd like to know if you intend, at this stage, to completely stop funding until a proper investigation is conducted. if not, why? if so, when exactly will you do it? is it correct and appropriate that the department which owns the company is also the authority which issues the licence and which, at the base, receives the funds from the eu and channels them to a company which it owns 100%? is that right and proper ? what do you think about that ? all community funding for the project must be stopped as it is in flagrant breach of eu directives. even the area designated by the commissioners as a special protection zone has been destroyed. compensation after the fact will not repair the damage done.
- mr president, by consolidating existing directives, this framework directive establishes the basic principles for a sustainable water policy in the european union. it provides a framework for the protection and management of our waters - surface, ground, estuary and coastal waters - through the establishment of river basin districts at national level and, in the case of ireland, at cross-border level. ireland broadly supports the common position and many of the amendments of the european parliament. a 25-year grace period for legislation, for example, is hardly acceptable. we have no difficulty with the polluter-pays principle in recovering the costs of water use at industrial, commercial and agricultural levels. however, i firmly believe that since water is synonymous with life and access to clean drinking water is a fundamental right, this directive should allow for a free basic supply of household water, a quota per household for drinking, cooking and sanitation. of course, we have to pay for waste and expensive domestic use and the cost recovery can be balanced accordingly. the introduction of water meters is viewed with great suspicion in ireland where we do not pay a bill for domestic water and therefore have not had water meters since 1977. meters could be considered as a very important instrument of water management by new water districts, with a view to eliminating water wastage and planning future requirements correctly. the aim of this directive is to promote sustainable water use within the eu. water is a scarce and precious resource. ideally, i would like amendment no 45 to be withdrawn tomorrow so that the common position on article 9 will be confined to water pricing. this would make it possible to apply subsidiarity to a cost policy on water pricing, with each member state drawing up its own plan, provided that the principles of this directive relating to incentives for sustainable and efficient water use are fully protected. if amendment no 45 is not withdrawn, i shall have to table an oral amendment tomorrow to ensure that member states can grant derogations from the provisions of this article to allow a minimum level of domestic water use which does not involve any contribution to the recovery of service costs linked to water use. all legislation must be applicable and based on the general principle of political acceptance. i believe that free access to clean drinking water is a fundamental right in developed countries as well as in developing countries.

in writing. - i want my concerns about the lienemann report and the reasons for my vote to be officially noted. we all agree that adequate controls must be put in place to protect our water resources and also water consumers. the approach suggested by the rapporteur is very welcome. however, taxes or prices for water consumption should be decided by the member states. in my view, it is not wise to use a tool as crude as a common price at european level for this issue. in ireland, all the major water users in the commercial, industrial and agricultural sectors are already paying for their water consumption. this decision was taken by the local government and the national government, and it should remain their decision in the future. these are the main reasons for my vote.
in writing. - the european union is currently adopting new eu water legislation to update and establish a long-term strategy to ensure the supply of drinking water and the protection of existing water resources, including rivers and coastal waters. i support the objectives of this community action in the field of water policy. however, i do not support the proposal that household water users should pay for the water they use. water, in this context, is a commodity. it is essential to life itself and people must have free access to sufficient resources for normal daily life.
- mr president, mr president-in-office, commissioner, in my view the eu member states' approach to geneva should be to give priority to the situations in kosovo, mexico, saudi arabia, sierra leone and china. as far as china is concerned, we should not give up on the subject just because discussions on the subject might be blocked. however, it is imperative that the eu speaks and acts in unison, and the threat of trade sanctions should strengthen our resolve rather than weaken it. however, if we are to give full expression to the decisions that may eventually be taken in geneva, then the office of the high commissioner for human rights must be given the necessary resources. the financial resources of the united nations are tightly allocated and, as a result, many of its vital programs depend on voluntary contributions for their survival. resources are also needed to promote practical cooperation and human rights, strategic planning, priority setting and enhanced communications between the donor community and un member states. to this end, the high commissioner recently launched an annual appeal for us$53 million to enable her office to carry out these tasks. the european union and its member states should take the initiative and contribute to a large extent to the allocation of these funds. in doing so, we would not only embarrass the united states, which should be taking its responsibilities in relation to the funding of the united nations, but we would also demonstrate that our commitment to the defence of human rights is more than just rhetorical.
counsel. - (pt) as regards the question by the honourable member, i should like to stress first of all that the task of the court of auditors is defined by the treaty establishing the european economic community. in accordance with article 246 of the treaty, the court of auditors shall examine the accounts of all revenue and expenditure of the community and the legality and regularity of the underlying transactions and shall ensure sound financial management. as is also clear from article 248. the court of auditors therefore has all the instruments necessary to carry out its task. as regards the honourable member's suggestion to increase the resources of the court of auditors, the council considers that all the institutions of the union have the financial means within the community budget to enable them to carry out their respective tasks properly. it would probably be desirable in some cases to increase these resources, but there are budgetary limitations which the honourable member is well aware of. i would like to take this opportunity to remind you that the budget of the european union is approved by common accord of the council and this parliament. the council therefore has no power to alter the task entrusted to the court of auditors by the treaty.
- if the court of auditors were to receive additional resources, this would be a wise expenditure as it would ensure that eu funding would not benefit from something that would be in contradiction with environmental concerns. for years the council has blocked the evaluation of the impact of the strategic development of plans and programmes. i would ask you, now that you are president of the council, whether you will make any progress on this extremely important proposal. secondly, since there is no mention of the structural funds in the current draft, can you make sure that they are included again? i would like to know what you could do about it as the current president-in-office of the council, because the council has been blocking this for a long time. this is very important. it would be very important for the court of auditors to be able to assess on a regular basis potential conflicts between environmental concerns and structural development, because the court of auditors could do remarkable work. his reports are extremely detailed and very important. this is the way to avoid such conflicts in the future.

- mr president, let me first of all congratulate the rapporteur on his excellent explanatory statement which highlights some of the serious shortcomings of the proposal. the lack of linguistic clarity will inevitably lead to legal uncertainties. but in such crucial matters, legal certainty is essential. because of its poor quality, the convention can lead to very different interpretations. moreover, it ignores the strict conditions to which interception is supposed to be subject under the european convention on human rights. article 18 authorises interception in another member state without the consent or authorisation of the latter. not only should this article be deleted, but everything else about the interception. the fbi took the initiative in establishing user requirements which form the basis of the current legislation. the council's proposal containing these requirements, enfopol 98, was approved by this parliament in the schmid report last may. today we have to provide the legal basis for interception and it is the combination of these two documents that should be considered. the requirements propose to allow the authorities to have access to the infrastructures of all service providers, i.e. fixed and mobile telephony services, the internet, etc. given the current poor quality of the project, this leaves the door open to abuse and leads me to ask the following question: since the user requirements specify that the police have access to all service providers without exception, does this also concern the internal service of the european parliament? are we passing rules that will allow police access to our own systems on the most petty of grounds? furthermore, the proposal does not define a threshold for the seriousness of offences. again, this leaves the door open to abuse. as regards the reversal of the burden of proof, the presumption of innocence is essential and is recognised in international law. the proposal does not guarantee that. neither the preamble nor the body of the text takes proper account of the rights of the defence, a basic principle of international law. this is a very serious matter. we are very concerned about the way things are going: the absence of a real public debate in national parliaments or elsewhere, the non-involvement of civil liberties organisations in europe who are very concerned about these developments.
in writing. - commissioner prodi stressed that 'africa is our responsibility' and not apparently that of the us or other major countries. i worked in africa 30 years ago. the country was poor and underdeveloped, but the problems are now more catastrophic. last year, two million people died of aids in africa and about 200,000 in armed conflicts. in zambia, 25 percent of teachers have died of aids. in some countries, children are less likely to have access to basic education and health care than they were 20 years ago. the actions we are taking in africa are inconsistent. we are seeing a huge duplication of effort. in addition, the specter of debt remains an obstacle to real development. there is no doubt that africa has become the forgotten continent.
in writing. - the united nations aids programme estimated at the end of 1998 that a total of 33.4 million people were infected with hiv worldwide. more than 95% of them live in developing countries, and sub-saharan africa is the most affected region in the world. while only 10% of the population lives in this region, 70% of all people infected in 1998 came from this region. 80% of the 13.9 million aids victims are from this region. the aids problem in africa is not just a matter of private or public health, but also of development. the effects of the aids pandemic mean that any progress towards development in the sub-saharan region is being neutralized or reversed. from the data i am basing myself on today, it is clear that this disease is taking a disproportionate toll on the developing countries least able to respond to its wide-ranging impacts. it is clear that the aids epidemic in sub-saharan africa is linked to poverty. aids treatment, which can cost $18,000 per person per year, is simply not affordable in countries where the annual health budget can be less than $5 per person. it is up to the european union and the united nations to tackle this social and fatal problem from a global and strategic perspective. first, we must ensure that the basic needs of the poorest people in the countries most severely affected by aids - ethiopia, lesotho, mozambique, tanzania, uganda and zambia - are fully met. prevention must start in primary school in these countries. primary school teachers who are best at the information they are trying to communicate must convey relevant messages about the health effects of aids to schoolchildren. health facilities and ngo staff must be given support and resources to treat patients effectively and adequately. it is essential that these organisations can obtain the limited resources they need to ensure the effectiveness of their action. at a more central level, there is a need to improve coordination between state donors, international and local ngos and private sector initiatives, so as not to waste scarce resources. already weak economies are still weakened by aids. resources that are already scarce are still becoming scarce. in countries most affected by the aids virus, at least 50% of hospital beds are occupied by people infected or suffering from an hiv-related illness. i'm not going to do that. i would like to support cuba's accession as an acp country under the new lomé convention which will soon be concluded between the european union and the african, caribbean and pacific countries. i know that this is a controversial point, particularly because of the attitude of the united states towards the castro regime. however, on this occasion the european union will insist on the introduction of political criteria in the lomé convention. good governance criteria covering the issues of refugees, corruption, human rights and democracy will be integrated into the final agreement between the european union and all the signatory states to the lomé convention belonging to the african, caribbean and pacific region.

reasonable that we should have european regulations on this. in ireland, some services are provided by companies outside ireland: for example, it is certain that british companies are working there. european directives. this has not been done in ireland, although some liberalisation has occurred without being hampered by the national government. a small segment of our market has been taken over by private operators. but the need for common legislation is underlined by the fact that the netherlands has liberalized, germany has not, and german companies enjoying the protection of national law are trying to gain market share in the netherlands. this is another example of the importance of advancing common legislation and ensuring that it is implemented throughout the union. i'm from a rural area. i support the principle of liberalisation and the right of everyone to compete and i recognise the benefits that common regulation and liberalisation have brought over the years. for example, it has enabled us to fly from peripheral areas to the heart of the union for a fraction of what it cost 20 years ago. i remember the time when people came to press us in this house to say that in the national interest we should not allow liberalization. we have allowed it and the result is that our youth can travel and enrich their education. our older citizens, even if they are dependent on social security, can enjoy the benefits of travel. liberalisation has therefore removed many of the pests in many areas and has enabled many people to enjoy a higher standard of living. on the other hand, it is not unlimited free trade and if you are from a really rural area you will readily admit that in a free trade situation some services cannot and will not be provided. in making its new proposals, the commission must therefore bear in mind these sparsely populated rural areas and not introduce into this legislation any element which will deprive these areas of the possibility of having such services. in rural areas for many years, even before the irish free state was born, we sometimes had a post office. today, it is probably the only public service in many remote areas and people fear that european legislation will eventually make it difficult to maintain it. i would like to stress that the existing legislation protects rather than threatens the right of every citizen living in very remote areas to have access to such a service. but some people complain that in ireland, social benefits are paid at post offices. i would like the commission to admit that this is a necessary social service. it should go hand in hand with our postal services in rural areas. we must implement the point stressed in the directive: a postal service accessible to everyone.
commission - i thank mrs maes not only for the statement she has just made but also for the continuing commitment she has shown to change since she has been in this house. i would like to assure him that our behaviour is not guided by any desire to get rid of certain tasks. we are focusing on the essential tasks, on the priorities, not to avoid any obligation, but to ensure absolutely that we can fully fulfil the core obligations we have. one of those obligations is very clear - and i agree with mrs maes here - it is to prepare for a genuine, smooth and peaceful enlargement, an enlargement which will serve the mutual interests of both the peoples of the acceding countries and the citizens of the present union. to ensure that we are able to fulfil this absolutely critical role of agents of the widest possible enlargement is one of the reasons why we have to make sure that we focus very strongly and very intensely on our priorities. let me tell him that the strategic planning and programming mechanisms, facilitated by the activity-based management, which we are about to introduce, may seem considerably arid and completely devoid of any idealism. having been brought up with the idea that the victory of ideals must be organised, i am probably in a position to invite him to understand and support our mechanistic view on this matter as it goes in the direction of the great central objectives of the union and its enlargement. i hope that, in terms of the degree of cooperation and partnership we will share with the council and parliament on all aspects of this policy, we will not only get the political development implemented quickly in the most efficient way possible, but also that we will be able to do so through coordination at the right time, which will be very beneficial for the union. i really believe that the reform, with all that it contains, including the new mechanisms, will facilitate such changes very strongly.
