- i would like to start by congratulating mrs attwooll on the preparation and presentation of her report. i welcome the establishment of a community framework for the collection and management of fisheries data which i believe is absolutely essential if we are to properly assess stock levels of the various fish species. of course, it is important that the data be both biological and economic in nature and that the information to be provided by the various member states be standardized at community level and, as the rapporteur has stated in his report, comply with international provisions. i welcome the fact that funding for national programmes is available, even if the minimum programme requirements are taken into account. i agree with the proposals that information given to international organisations should also be given to the commission. otherwise, the objective of collecting and collating data would be defeated and, if the information is not available, the precautionary principle would continue to apply. sound and factual information is of the greatest interest to the sector. i believe that the streamlined information that will be available will be of crucial benefit to policy-makers and their advisers and will be of vital importance to them. in addition, they will help to ensure a more sustainable fisheries, which will continue to provide jobs in regions of the union where there is no alternative source of jobs. i am pleased to confirm that in my country there is a commitment to ensure that the practical details, logistics and use of information are developed in the best interests of the sector's resources and decision-makers and our group has no difficulty in supporting these reports.
-en) i would like to congratulate mrs attwooll and say that i agree with most of what she said. i consider it a weakness of the proposal that reporting is not compulsory in all member states. i cannot understand a proposal to draw up a regulation which some member states could evade without incurring a fine, nor can i understand that within a european union which is only 50% self-sufficient in fisheries, there is highly sensitive information and that by drawing up the necessary reports the national or regional interests of certain states or fishing communities are put at risk. it is important, in view of the next review of the policy in 2002, that we have all the information possible, both economic and biological. in the past, someone told me that i had defended the decisions taken by the commission. it is not easy to defend the european union in the fisheries sector, in the coastal areas of the union. however, i defended these decisions because they were based on properly substantiated scientific knowledge. however, i learn, as i look more closely at the problem, that the information is actually quite scattered, coming from here and there, and that no clear economic and biological picture emerges. if we do not get this picture, it will not be possible to take reasonable decisions on the future of this policy within two years. it's not easy. i could speak for all the people in my constituency who live in a coastal area and say that they all agree on one thing, namely that the european union is the source of all their problems. well, i don't agree with them, but i think if we don't base our information and our decisions on good science and if everyone isn't subject to the same obligation to share it, then we're going to make bad decisions.
- mr piétrasanta explained why we support certain aspects of the commission's proposal, such as the development of a vaccine and compensation for fishermen who are forced to slaughter their fish, but we are not at all satisfied with the idea of changes in the slaughter policy. some people in this house have said that norway has done a better job in controlling the ibs epidemic and that the proposed changes will bring us closer to the norwegian method, but it must be said that there is more than one difference between norwegian and community policy. it is a community policy which will also affect ireland - my home country - should unfortunate events of the same kind occur. there are other differences in policy regarding disease surveillance, calving periods, movement restrictions on contaminated fish and materials, etc. before we rush to copy the norwegian policy of slaughter, we should try to apply these other measures to see if we can limit the disaster. in addition, none of them have mentioned bse in the past. for many years the greens have been calling for the bse issue to be addressed. if this had been done at that time, we could have saved a very large number of farmers and saved the eu a lot of money. this is also a matter of environmental protection. the protection of the operators themselves and the protection of public health.
the rapporteur. - the proposal under consideration is very important, as its introduction emphasizes. i am sure that my colleagues in the committee on fisheries and in parliament are already aware of our commitment to technical conservation measures based on sound technical advice. the organisation responsible for providing such advice based on sound scientific evidence is the international commission for the conservation of atlantic tunas. this international fisheries organisation, of which the european union is a member, is responsible for the conservation of tuna and tuna-like species in the atlantic and surrounding seas, including the mediterranean. the commission was set up in march 1969 and is officially supposed to meet every two years, but in practice it does so every year and takes its decisions on the basis of scientific advice. the organisation then makes recommendations for the conservation of the stocks. the organisation has four standing committees, composed of national experts, covering the fields of research and statistics, finance and administration, compliance and conservation and management measures. there are also four working groups for tropical tuna, northern and southern temperate tuna and other species. these working groups are empowered to initiate regulatory measures in respect of the species covered by the convention. the proposals from these working parties are submitted to the commission, which then makes recommendations to the contracting parties. these recommendations are binding, but the contracting parties have a six-month period in which to object. as a contracting party, the european union is obliged to transpose such recommendations into community law. at its meeting in santiago de compostela in november 1998, iccat recommended certain amendments to the rules on minimum landing sizes for bluefin tuna. the iccat also issued recommendations in 1974, 1994 and 1996. it is now forbidden to land or sell bluefin tuna aged 0 , weighing of course less than 1.8 kilos. at its november 1998 session, the ices standing committee on research and statistics was asked to undertake a new assessment of the western and eastern atlantic and mediterranean bluefin tuna stocks. the committee would therefore provide the organisation with the information necessary to review, develop and improve the recovery plans. it was decided that the cprs should present a study during the 1998 session, highlighting various possible scenarios for stock regeneration, at levels which can produce maximum sustainable yield, taking into account the various variations in possible levels of recruitment and stock mixing. in addition, the cprs study should indicate the monthly rotating-seine fences, so that the fences are more effective for the conservation of bluefin tuna. as a result of this research, the session agreed that instead of a blanket ban on the rotating-net tuna fishery in the mediterranean during the period from 1 to 31 august, a more selective approach should be implemented. it is important to protect the tuna fishery for future generations. bluefin tuna is the most expensive fish in the world, with a daily sale price of up to €90,000 per tonne in tokyo, and of course, the importance of mediterranean tuna is indicated by the fact that they account for 70% of all landings in the region, estimated at nearly 30,000 tonnes. of this, 80% is caught by the rotating net. the mediterranean is an important spawning area for bluefin tuna, so it is important to take the necessary measures to strengthen the protection of juveniles. we must respect the minimum size and implement technical measures if we are to eliminate catches of fish of age 0. the measures adopted in november 1998 came into force on 21 june, i repeat, on 21 june 1999, almost nine months ago, several months before the communication was sent to the committee on fisheries. i think, commissioner, that this is not enough and that in the future we will have to learn from it. the reports must be submitted to parliament's committee on fisheries before decisions are taken, otherwise we are just an approval body. i know that is not the intention of the commission or the commissioner, but we must learn from the mistakes of the past.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's environmental policy is not distorted. - the committee on the environment generally welcomes the commission's communication and also welcomes the fact that the committee on fisheries has adopted all our amendments. it is now widely accepted that the effects of fishing on the marine environment go far beyond the simple capture of target species, accompanied by a few by-catches. we welcome the commission's intention to integrate environmental protection into other eu policies, including the common fisheries policy. after all, the treaties have provided since 1992 that the precautionary principle and environmental protection must be integrated into community policies. but, as has been pointed out, it is essential to adopt a rigorous definition of the precautionary principle, because it is far too easy to declare oneself in favour of this idea while maintaining the same habits. the 1995 un fisheries agreement could be a good model in this respect. the commission communication does not, however, address the question of monitoring and control. the best policies and regulations in the world will not be enough to ensure the conservation of the marine environment without an effective system to ensure that they are respected. the member states are responsible for monitoring and the council recently adopted a list of serious infringements with a view to achieving a certain degree of harmonisation of the penalties applied in the various countries for the same infringements. this is a good step forward, but as the commission and the committee on fisheries have repeatedly stressed, the monitoring programmes of all the member states need to be significantly improved. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on his excellent report.

- madam president, today's debate is necessary and timely. we all agree that the european union, despite the economic recovery, is functioning in a way that is very unsatisfactory for too many citizens; and, to be more precise, it doesn't work at all for 15 million of them. it is an affront to individual and collective dignity, which gives us at least 15 million reasons to carry out the project you have set out for us today, mr prime minister. i would also like to thank you for coming to this parliament to discuss this with us before you go to your summit meeting. the potential is clear: if the european union could adopt best practice today, like its member states and its competitors, it would have 30 million additional jobs, twice the number of unemployed today. it is a practical measure of potential, if we can adopt the right policies and follow the right methods. the task of lisbon, as you have very rightly pointed out, mr prime minister and mr prodi, is to bring about a new practice and a new paradigm in european politics. the eldr group believes that the role of the union at this stage is to develop this new paradigm in such a way as to enable europe and our citizens to reach their full potential. the eu can play an important role in creating new economic momentum through greater consistency in benchmarking, sharing of best practice and improved strategic cooperation and coordination. too many european markets remain too fragmented. there are not many successful european smes. europe has too few entrepreneurs, too few companies. you yourself have observed, mr prime minister, that there was too little venture capital available on the european capital markets. we are not doing enough in research and development, either individually as states or collectively as europe. and only a tiny fraction of what we do is turned into products and services. the lisbon challenge will be to identify policy and methods that can address these shortcomings and unlock this potential dynamic. it will be a real empowerment exercise. the jobs we will be able to offer european citizens will be our best weapon against social exclusion. the new paradigm must also be absolutely people-centred, reflecting the concept of lifelong learning, investing in the capacity to adapt through education and training and in personal development and individual perspectives. mr barón crespo described it as a social democratic project. i'd rather describe it as an enlightened liberal project. in the context of budgetary constraints and sound macroeconomic policy - and i emphasize this as president prodi did before me - our european social models offer, strictly speaking, a social security system. but for too many excluded and longterm unemployed, young people, women and other marginalized groups, passive social dependency is not enough. she's a trap. we must unleash this new dynamic at the individual level by affirming that we have the necessary resources, even in the context of financial consolidation. but these resources are being used passively and must be transformed into active models of social assistance to encourage all those who can to take action and all those who cannot to continue to be protected. it is by having a job-creating economy and sustainable social assistance models that we can best combat unemployment and promote social cohesion in europe. finally, as regards the knowledge society, it is clear that we must transform our thinking not only at a technological level but also at a social level. it goes without saying that we need to transform the old ways of social spending into new economic and social opportunities. we must get rid of the policies and methods which have condemned a generation of europeans to being second-class players on the world stage. we can achieve this by enabling europe and its citizens to reach their full potential. if the lisbon summit achieves the objectives it has set out today, it will have taken an important step towards the dynamic i have mentioned. - i would like to join my colleagues in congratulating the two rapporteurs, even though i do not entirely agree with their comments and suggestions. admittedly, the task they have been given, to try to square a circle that cannot be, is impossible. in view of the very short time available, i would like to focus on a specific point raised by the previous speaker and others. this is the potential conflict which could bring this charter into conflict with the european convention on human rights. let us not forget that each member state has ratified the european convention on human rights, has incorporated it into its national legislation and is obliged to respect the decisions of the european court of human rights in strasbourg. over the last 50 years, the european convention, thanks to the decisions of the court, has developed and evolved to adapt to the new rights and freedoms that have developed with society. if the member states do not know, if the law is interpreted strictly, all eu legislation supersedes any constitutional or other national legislative provision in all member states. if the charter were to be incorporated into the treaties, it would replace the european convention on human rights, all national constitutional provisions and also all national legislative provisions. while it is important that each of us uphold the inalienable freedoms and rights enjoyed by each citizen and group of citizens, i believe that incorporating the convention into community law and applying it at member state level would be a better way of solving the problem than a charter.
- it goes without saying that many people, including those who elected me, fear that this charter will override national constitutions, particularly with regard to the support and protection of fundamental institutions such as the family, marriage and the protection of life before and after birth. it should be remembered that europe has shared a common position throughout its history on the importance of supporting and protecting the family and marriage. these principles are enshrined in all our constitutions and those of the countries applying for membership of the european union. i can show you the constitutions of post-communist states, where family and marriage are protected. i am glad that our charter refers to family life. article 9 (1) states: 'everyone has the right to found a family'. it says it is based on article 12 of the european convention on human rights, which states that 'married men and women have the right to marry and to found a family in accordance with national laws'. society recognizes marriage not for moral reasons but because it accepts, on a rational basis, the social and economic stability it provides for society and the perpetuation of our species. in the interest of best practice - and although it is becoming increasingly difficult to define the family and we need a lot of sensitivity and compassion in this area - we should not reject the traditional family unit in favour of the basic unity we are striving to achieve. on the contrary, we should support it. article 9 (3) says: 'the union shall ensure the protection of children'. she suggests that we add: 'in accordance with the united nations convention on the rights of the child'. i agree, but i would strongly suggest that we add to paragraph 3 that the union will support the family first and foremost as the primary and natural educator and protector of the child, as is its duty. the union should only intervene if the family is unable to play its role. as the convention states, 'the family as the fundamental group of society and the natural environment for the growth and well-being of its members, especially children, should be afforded the necessary protection and assistance to fulfil its full responsibility within the community'.
- mr president, waste treatment is now a central element in the development of the highest standards of environmental protection. unfortunately, our consumer society is contributing to the increase in household waste and industrial waste is a growing concern. fortunately, people are now aware of the importance of protecting the natural environment and i believe that the economic and social climate is conducive to achieving this objective. in previous debates i have mentioned the need to reduce waste: in this respect, food and consumer goods producers have an important role to play, particularly with regard to packaging in its various forms and the importance of using products compatible with recycling, which is of course my preferred option. we are all aware of the need to reduce the amount of waste that is currently being disposed of in landfills and every effort must be made to find solutions that are more environmentally friendly. i do not think we will be able to eliminate landfills completely in the foreseeable future. however, we must not only reduce the volume of waste going to landfill, but also be more selective about the materials that are disposed of. while incineration is getting very bad press - and recent scheduling decisions in ireland have certainly raised the crucial question of the location of incinerators - it is difficult to envisage a complete waste disposal programme without incineration, particularly with regard to toxic waste, and in this respect consideration should be given to finding locations where the installations will not have a negative impact on the environment. in conclusion, mr president, it is clear that we need to have more informed debate to help alleviate the concerns of citizens about the health aspects of incineration, which has been addressed through extensive research in various member states and through the experience gained in the state-of-the-art facilities currently operating throughout europe.

in writing. - mr blokland rightly expresses serious concerns about waste disposal, including incineration. i come from a country where 90% of waste is buried, and i come to the conclusion that well-managed incineration is both necessary and unavoidable. the irish government's commitment to recycling and careful separation of waste is far from clear. separation of waste, including the use of organic waste for compost, should be a priority in any waste management strategy.
in writing. - in recent years, the european union has been at the forefront of devising new waste management strategies. in fact, the directive we are discussing today aims to mitigate the negative effects of waste incineration on the environment and human health by significantly reducing the levels of emissions of several major air pollutants. this directive also seeks to monitor the discharge of waste into our water systems. the report has come back to us on second reading. i take note of one of the european council recommendations: any non-hazardous waste incineration company intending to incinerate hazardous waste must apply for an integrated pollution prevention and control permit. i think that is a particularly reasonable proposal. it is very important that the european union plays a very active role in the development of new waste management proposals. the eu generates 2 billion tonnes of waste every year, or more than 1 kg per person per day. on the issue of waste management, the european union agrees on one point: the future of waste disposal is not in the hands of the local authorities of the country seeking new landfills within its borders. the environmental consequences of landfilling are a growing concern. the only viable solution is to abandon landfills altogether in favour of more integrated waste management programmes. the european union fully supports the strict, ambitious but achievable targets set by the irish government for recycling and recovery of waste. the targets for the next 15 years are as follows: - 50% of all household waste currently sent to landfills is diverted from its intended destination; - a minimum of 65% reduction in biodegradable waste currently sent to landfills; - the installation of waste recovery infrastructure, using environmentally friendly technologies capable of treating up to 300 000 tonnes of biodegradable waste per year. all these measures reflect the changing public opinion on the issue of waste management. the era of landfills is coming to an end. they must be replaced by more environmentally friendly technologies. this is a position unanimously supported by the 15 member states of the european union.

- mr president, i would like to join the previous speakers in congratulating the rapporteur. he approached the matter with the conviction and the great devotion to which they have accustomed us. i was pleased to hear him say that there was no real conflict on the principles and objectives of the proposal for a directive. i have no doubt that the commissioner will make his contribution to a satisfactory solution, as parliament and the commission share a common objective in this very important and crucial area. the directive on intra-community trade in bovine animals is vital to the livestock farmers' economy, particularly in my country, ireland, which exports 90% of its total livestock production - a very large proportion of which is exported to continental europe and beyond. in this respect, i support the proposals to update the 1997 directive again, provided that this is done in a rational way. we must eliminate unnecessary bureaucracy, while providing full traceability of animal movements and ensuring public health.
- ireland's interest in this particular amending directive relates to a request by ireland to allow pre-export tuberculosis testing to take place in collection centres rather than just in farms of origin, as is the case in ireland today. i understand that we have almost two years to discuss with the commission any difficulties we may have in this area and i am glad to see the commissioner here today. in accordance with the union trade rules and in the interests of food safety and consumer confidence in the animal health status, annual testing is mandatory for the maintenance of the officially tuberculosis-free status of our bovine herds. in ireland, with a cattle herd of between 7 and 8 million head, nearly 10.5 million tests for tuberculosis have been carried out annually in recent years on 138 000 cattle herds. since 1954, when our tuberculosis regime was introduced, we have spent €2.54 billion in ireland - a small country - on tuberculosis tests and then on brucellosis tests. i think this shows the determination of the irish people to protect the health status of their cattle herds. following the detection of a positive reaction, a special contiguous testing programme is set up for flocks adjacent to flocks subject to restriction. the analysis of the test results is carried out in a monitored manner by a special tuberculosis research unit. routine post-mortem examinations are carried out by ministry veterinarians on all slaughtered animals. animals which test positive for tuberculosis are slaughtered. from the very beginning of the bovine tuberculosis eradication regime, the ministry of agriculture has recognised the benefit of pre-movement testing and we are asking for our amendment to the amending directive to be welcomed. i would like the commissioner to consider carefully the following point: the only test provided for in the eu and by irish legislation is the only comparative intradermal test as defined in ec directive 64/432. i believe that considerable effort has been made in the area of laboratory blood tests for tuberculosis. the interferon gamma test is the most promising and is used in practice as a complement to tuberculination of bovine herds. i would like the commissioner to tell us where we are and when we will have a blood test that is accepted by the eu. the tb test we now use is specific to the herd, not the animal. we have to get to the point where individual blood tests are in place.

in writing. - i would like to state the reasons why i felt obliged to abstain in the final vote, even though, like my colleagues, i fully agree with the need to protect fundamental freedoms and rights and to ensure that future social developments are taken into account. the member states of the european union have already ratified and transposed into their national legislation the european convention on human rights, which, although nearly 50 years old today, has proved capable of adapting to changing conditions and the emergence of new rights. i believe that what is now being proposed to us with this 'charter' will ultimately lead to conflicts of interpretation of rights between the different documents, with the risk of legal voids and, ironically, of damaging existing rights. we need more time to examine these risks of conflict.

in writing. - on the whole, this is an excellent report, with the exception of paragraphs 52 and 56. these are ill-formulated and ambiguous. they deal with matters which are undoubtedly within the competence of each member state and should remain so.
- mr president, the un sanctions have become a weapon of mass destruction, killing at least 200 children every day. dennis halliday, who resigned as head of the un humanitarian mission in iraq in protest against the sanctions, confirms the unicef statistics: five to six thousand children die every month as a direct result of the sanctions. mary robinson, the un high commissioner for human rights, spoke out against the sanctions imposed in violation of international law, which prohibits starving civilians as a method of warfare. she said: "how can you expect me to condemn human rights violations in algeria, china and elsewhere, when the un itself is responsible for the situation in iraq?" because of the economic sanctions imposed by the united nations, the iraqi people have no means left to rebuild the infrastructure, leaving the ground free for infectious diseases caused by water and air pollution. moreover, these diseases, which can be cured, kill for lack of medical and food assistance. the united nations estimates that five to six thousand children die every month because of sanctions, and this despite the oil-for-food program. sanctions have the capacity to kill relentlessly and it is innocent iraqi civilians caught in the crossfire who are paying the price. this has to stop. in his documentary broadcast two weeks ago on british television, john pilger described the situation very clearly. anyone with a shred of humanity cannot turn a blind eye to such a situation and cannot accept the persecution of innocent people who have no influence over saddam hussein. it is ridiculous that children who are not allowed to speak are victims of war because, basically, the americans, and indeed the british, support such a display of cruelty against their peers.
- yesterday evening, after the house voted, a comment was made on which i wish to distance myself and to which i do not wish to be associated. there was no possible misinterpretation and the members of this house did indeed call the technical group a racist technical group. that was an unfair comment. no one, even with the most fervent imagination, could call mrs emma bonino a racist. members of this house should be extremely careful in the way they treat their colleagues. some people may not like the fact that this technical group has been set up, but that is a fact. these members were elected, as was i, and as was every member of this house. this is a reality we must accept. everyone has rights and i don't think the comment made yesterday is acceptable.
- mr president, i welcome this measure. from an administrative point of view, the formalization is a positive point. but after careful consideration, the amount is relatively small - $25 million over a six-year period. given the size of the agriculture budget and the fact that it will be spread across the union, this is a relatively small sum. i therefore welcome the fact that the funding rate for the bulk of this amount is 50% rather than 75%. the more of this is financed at a rate of less than 50%, the further the money goes and the greater the impact. the common agricultural policy is the subject of much criticism. one of the points which has been understood only to a lesser extent is the importance of this policy from a social point of view; the fact that in all the union's declining regions the common agricultural policy has contributed to maintaining the population and has provided social support for communities that could not have survived otherwise. this is particularly true in peripheral countries such as ireland, greece, spain and portugal, where communities facing extinction have been helped and sustained, if not permanently, at least certainly until a change in the economic environment occurs, until new technologies arrive. the long-term effect of the common agricultural policy will be far greater than citizens have often imagined. i have no criticism of the way the commission has acted in interaction with the agricultural industry over the years. in my personal experience, the european commission has been more open to approaches and information exchange with the agricultural industry than most of the ministries of agriculture in most member states. over the years, the commission has shown member states how to adopt an open attitude with very limited resources. however, there are new challenges, which are the result of consumer anxiety about new food production processes and systems. these issues must be explained not only to farmers but also to consumers. there is a very impressive mythology about what is healthy and what is not. we have an important task ahead of us, namely to explain the operation of the whole production system to european consumers and to consumers outside the union who will be future buyers of our food. it is important that we devote as much resources as possible to achieving this objective. the last point i want to make is that these issues will provoke a reaction from the agricultural world. given that we will gradually but inevitably reduce aid during the enlargement process and, at the same time, keep up the pressure to protect the environment and consumers, there will be friction between producers and the european administration. the vote on the largest possible number of items is therefore a wise policy.
- mr president, i have been watching this whole thing about the structural funds and the additionality for many years and there are a number of points where we could learn from the past. the first point i would like to make is that the funds available are, in absolute terms, relatively limited in most regions of the community and their impact is more political than economic - although this is also important. i have said many times that we should not simply draw up regulations at eu level telling the citizen what he should or should not do, but that we should work towards what european citizens and regions in the union consider to be tangible benefits. even rich regions will appreciate the fact that the european union is trying to help narrow the gap between the rich and the poor. a second point we can make on the macroeconomic level is that if portugal, greece or ireland are given a certain amount from the structural funds, as has been done in the past, this money is, in any case, transferred to these states. this is the first impact of this aid. the money is actually being transferred and, whether the letter of the law is being followed or not, these countries are benefiting. i would like to make another point, namely that the past can serve as a lesson. first of all, we have put some order into the regulations. however, originally these regulations were drafted to suit each sector and each political interest. it was therefore impossible, with the staff at the disposal of the european commission, to control how the money was spent. this is still the case to a large extent. we are now in the third or fourth programme. what we must be absolutely sure of is that we can learn from our past mistakes and avoid making them again in the future. if we are to be able to exercise really effective control over how this money is spent, we must first look at the amount of spending by these countries in previous years. they should also be asked to submit their plans to the european commission. the european commission will therefore have to identify the weaknesses in the plan and state how it wants the money it grants to be spent, in addition to the funds already allocated by these countries to the implementation of all their projects. in my personal experience in ireland, i have never heard the european commission reprimand ireland for shortcomings in the area of additionality. however, it should be remembered that the aid granted to ireland by the structural funds between 1982 and 1992 reached record amounts; the aid is less important at the moment, but there was a huge amount of money. however, from 1982 to 1992, the public investment programme actually declined, including the structural funds. it decreased in real terms by 30%. so where did the structural funds go? the structural funds have been used for public sector payroll and public health. whether or not these funds have been profitable to the irish economy over these years is debatable, as they have contributed to inflation and the demand for higher wages. there are, therefore, lessons to be learned: the commission's staff to deal with the irish problem - three or four people - was compared to the 30,000 civil servants in ireland, and the public administration was winning every time!
- mr president, the genesis of regulation 925 of 1999, to which the resolution we are considering refers, gives me some problems. the previous parliament approved it without any debate. this is contrary to the principle of non-discrimination. this is a breach of the fundamental requirement of justification under article 253 of the treaty in that the regulation contains no explanation for using the dilution ratio of an aircraft engine as a criterion where certification of emission and noise standards for aircraft engines already exists. this is contrary to the principle of proportionality and, more importantly, it is contrary to the wto rules which require the use of performance standards rather than design standards as is the case in this regulation. in whose interest has this legislation departed from the established procedures and practice of using objective performance criteria for environmental standards? this legislation puts to rest the proposal made by airbus at a meeting organised by airbus in toulouse on 29 and 30 september and 1 october 1997, legislation based on effective commercial sponsorship. at that meeting, the members of the european civil aviation commission (ecac) were told by their legal counsel that the icao agreement did not allow them to do as airbus requested, and airbus therefore persuaded the commission to legislate where the ecac could not. we now have legislation that threatens to provoke a new transatlantic trade war - but that is another story - that does not respect best practice with performance criteria and, surprisingly, excludes aircraft using a modern us-made engine while still allowing the use of the rolls royce engine which, conveniently, has a dilution ratio of 3.1. yes, mr president, noise pollution affects 20% of our population, and 0.01% comes from aircraft. i strongly support the standards necessary to protect the health and quality of life of our fellow citizens. the strictest possible criteria for reducing noise and emissions from aircraft engines can, however, only be achieved by using performance criteria rather than design criteria, which are too fast-tracked and commercially sponsored by anti-competitive legislation. environmental legislation based on design criteria is unprecedented, ladies and gentlemen, and rightly so. i would respectfully ask the commissioner, in the interests of fairness, to look into the details of the genesis of the regulation in question to ascertain its impact on the global aviation industry. i do not care whether it is america, europe, china or mars: global means fair. it should first and foremost serve the important environmental objectives it claims to achieve. i fully support the aim of this regulation and we must agree on a timetable for implementing noise standards, but it must be based on objective performance criteria and not on design criteria. the integrity of this house demands detailed answers to questions which raise serious doubts about aspects of a regulation on which the motion before us is based and on which we are asked to vote tomorrow.
- mr president, i would like to thank both rapporteurs for their work and say that they have reached an honourable consensus and produced good reports. i want to wish the new commissioner good luck with her first budget and i would like to say that i sincerely hope that the procedure will go smoothly and efficiently. i also hope that we will do everything in our power to produce a sensible budget in the most effective way possible. if problems should arise - and they do sometimes - they will be the proposal to finance the deficit in category 4 with appropriations from the agriculture section of the budget. the proposal is more likely to create psychological problems in rural areas of the european union than real problems. we must reassure european farmers that there are regulations in place, that they give them rights and that, whatever happens, it is impossible for the european union to renounce these obligations. in any case, the revenue shows a deficit. the commission has made a statement for the period 2000-06 which sets out certain limits within which agricultural expenditure would be expected. we are now hearing new proposals, in the event of enlargement, for entirely new policies for the new member states and in the absence of any provision on how they will be financed. we need a little more clarity and there is growing tension between the institutions on the question of the balkans. the sooner the commission can come up with clear figures for annual expenditure, the better. in fact, i do not think that it is necessary to draw on the agricultural part of the budget to finance a decent programme within the established amounts - namely eur 5.5 billion - given the obligations of the union, the sums that will be committed by the member states and what will be requested because of the situation in serbia. so why should we fight for some 300 million euros, which i think we will not have to spend on agriculture in the end?
- mr president, i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on his hard work and in particular on the excellent definition of renewable energy. this directive should only be intended to support new technologies which are not yet commercially viable. the directive, as some speakers have already stated, must have binding targets for member states and the eu as a whole to significantly increase the share of renewable energy in the overall energy market. i note that the commission is proposing a review in 2005, but i would urge you to ask the member states to draw up annual reports on the achievement of the objectives contained in the white paper. i would also like the directive not to impose any ceilings or limitations on the direct aid mechanisms chosen by the member states. its main objective should also be to encourage fair and non-discriminatory access to the network. the directive should also include mechanisms to discourage member states, as we have heard before, from granting market-distorting subsidies to traditional forms of energy, in particular fossil and nuclear fuels. the nuclear power sector has long benefited from hidden subsidies which should have been subject to a major review or even a complete ban long ago. the argument that support for renewable energy is a market distortion, in the context of the huge subsidies for nuclear and fossil fuels in the european union, is completely false. i hope the commissioner will take that very seriously. the directive should explicitly propose a system of support and benefits for small and medium-sized enterprises in the decentralised production and distribution of renewable energy. it should include incentives for member states to grant advantages to regions, in particular rural areas, in respect of labour-intensive organic production. the directive should propose - and this is extremely important - mechanisms for internalising the external costs of traditional energy production. in conclusion, i would like to stress that it is important that certain actions taken by member states to include renewable energy in the energy mix of the electricity sector are at least equivalent to the overall eu commitment in the white paper on renewable energy, as adopted by the council and parliament. i would mention in particular the unsatisfactory rate of penetration in the united kingdom, ireland and france. in this respect, we can clearly see from empirical studies that grid access is by far the best way to achieve high renewable energy percentages rather than the other methods applied in these countries.
- mr president, i think everyone agrees that the development of renewable energy sources is a step in the right direction. if we are serious about this, we must be prepared to allocate the necessary funds for research in this field, both at national and european level. parliament recently voted to maintain and increase funding for the save programme. in my own country, ireland, a total of €125 million has been spent on developing an environmentally sustainable energy sector. i am glad that the rapporteur identifies the link between renewable energy sources and wider policy considerations. the creation of an internal electricity market should provide electricity consumers with a real choice. the introduction of an element of competition in any sector should lead to more favourable prices for consumers. while it is desirable to harmonise the rules of the game, it may also be necessary to grant subsidies to certain types of energy in order to promote other objectives. the development of renewable energy is clearly linked to environmental objectives, as well as to employment, regional development and the promotion of local initiatives. i would like to see the development of small local initiatives that would strengthen rural development by providing, say, 20 to 30 jobs per project. such projects should not be open only to the business sector and local groups such as small locally based cooperatives should be supported in setting up such enterprises at the local level. the report states that aid for renewable energy projects should not be considered as direct aid under the state aid regulation. it is important that member states are allowed to continue to grant subsidies to renewable energy sources that are not yet economically viable. beyond the obvious environmental and other benefits, this may in fact be justified in terms of competition. it is clear that there are serious market distortions and many hidden subsidies, both for coal-fired and oil-fired power. it would be virtually impossible to quantify these subsidies, which have been ingrained in the industry over the decades of generator construction. we still have a major challenge: meeting our commitments to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from the energy sector, in line with the kyoto protocol, while promoting growth within our economies. while member states must work towards a 12% share for renewable energy, i believe that the imposition of a single binding target would not be useful, due to the significant differences between the energy market structures of the member states. everything i've said so far is on the balance sheet. however, to come to the liabilities, the report specifically calls for peat power plants to be excluded. the peat power stations are working very well in my country and are an important contribution to the energy supply of ireland. the report also calls for the introduction of a european energy tax. i am totally opposed to this approach and therefore reject this tax. i therefore regret to say that my final vote on this proposal will be strongly affected by this.
in writing. -the patentability of biotechnologies has been the subject of a long debate in this parliament. in this debate, i and other green members have warned of the consequences. i have warned that patenting human genes or parts of human genes would jeopardise freedom of research and hinder medical progress. genetic information can only be discovered, it cannot be invented. the commercialisation of the knowledge of the human genome, which has been accumulated by research in many parts of the world, would be an obstacle to the medical applications of this important scientific knowledge of the human body and its functions. the human genome and its sequences should not be patentable. the fact that the european patent office has granted such a patent is extremely shocking and this patent must be annulled.
in writing. -(en) i would first like to congratulate the people who drafted and voted directive 98/44/ec which represents a step forward in the protection of human beings. unfortunately, some companies have already begun developing and patenting cloning techniques. while declaring support for a ban on "reproductive cloning", they have already patented and commercialized closed human cells for the treatment of ageing-related diseases and degenerative conditions. a patent for these techniques was granted in the uk in august 1995. while accepting the argument that good faith cloning experiments can contribute to scientific progress that could alleviate human suffering, i must point out that in regard to the totally unacceptable use of human embryos at the embryonic cell stage, cultures "outside their natural environment", there is a loophole. these experiments, under the guise of "therapeutic cloning", are already patented and are currently being researched. in the light of these facts, i ask the commission to make a specific reference to the patenting of the human embryo at the embryonic cell stage.

- mr president, in january 1999 the euro became the new currency of most of the european union. since then, there is no longer any risk of exchange rate fluctuations and, in theory, the cost to consumers of money transfers from one country to another and of exchanging one euro area currency for another should have decreased sharply. provisions oblige financial institutions in the union to substantially reduce the cost of money transfers from one euro area country to another. it should be remembered that only last year the european consumers association, which represents consumers in the european union, carried out a major survey on the costs of cross-border payments. the conclusion of this survey clearly shows that for consumers the costs of transferring money from one country to another in europe are still too high. in the coming years, e-commerce will grow and high bank charges for foreign exchange will have a serious deterrent effect on consumers wishing to shop online. it is important to reassure consumers about electronic payments by establishing a legal framework. the survey also shows that consumers still have to pay much more for cross-border transfers than for domestic transfers. it doesn't make sense. the euro has removed the risks of exchange rate fluctuations and costs should have been significantly reduced. i support the directive on crossborder transfers currently in force in the union. in conclusion, i am convinced that the new directive on crossborder credit transfers is good news for consumers in the union. this is also good news for financial institutions.

- mr president, i support the enlargement of the european union because i know that it will consolidate the new democracies of central and eastern europe. i know that it will also increase the size of the economic bloc that is the european union, which will, over time, ensure that the union's competitiveness as an economic entity is strengthened. i know that there is a need for reforms within the existing european institutions. however, any changes to internal decision-making procedures must be balanced and fair. we do not want a two-speed europe in which the larger member states impose the pace at the expense of the smaller member states. for example, the report under discussion today calls for a system of rotation in the composition of the commission. this means that the automatic right of smaller member states to have a member of the commission will be restricted. i do not think that is a particularly sensible proposal. the commission has affinities with 380 million citizens of the union. the irish certainly recognise the importance of the commission as an institutional body within the european union. the commission also supervises the implementation of the competition policy, the common agricultural policy, the common fisheries policy and the common transport policy. the union must get closer to its citizens. we should also remember that some member states, including my own country, are due to hold a referendum on any changes to the treaties arising from the next igc. the loss of the automatic right to appoint a commissioner would certainly be a very difficult, if not impossible, proposition to make to the irish electorate in this referendum. i would not like to return to the question of extending the qualified majority voting system at european council level. the council represents the interests of the governments of the 15 member states within the institutional framework of the union and currently, in accordance with the existing eu treaties, the unanimous agreement of the 15 member states is required for any change in taxation within the union. the report under discussion today seeks to extend qualified majority voting to all legislative activities, with the exception of those of a constitutional nature. i believe that the member states should retain the right of veto in tax matters. the irish tax system is innovative in many respects and has contributed to the current growth rates. i do not think that the extension of qualified majority voting in the council on taxation is a particularly welcome development.
in writing. - the european parliament will vote on 12 april on a new directive on the voluntary release of genetically modified organisms (gmos). there are crucial issues at stake. it is time to hold those who are engaged in the deliberate dissemination of genetically modified organisms legally responsible for the damage caused to human health and the environment. they should be held liable for any damage caused by such dissemination and they should be obliged to take out liability insurance, just as every driver should take out liability insurance, in case he kills or injures another person. arguments have been made that this issue should rather be covered by horizontal legislation. it should be noted, however, that this argument has been known to us since the discussions on directive 90/220/eec began in 1989. at that time the commission promised that civil liability for environmental damage caused by gmos would be covered by horizontal legislation, but we have been waiting for more than ten years for this promise to be fulfilled. we must adopt an immediate and categorical ban on the use of antibiotic resistance marker genes. many scientists and organisations, such as the pasteur institute and the british medical association, have called for a ban on the use of antibiotic resistance marker genes, which have no particular function in plants and are therefore superfluous. the common position states that antibiotic resistance marker genes would only be "gradually eliminated" in the future and only if "they are likely to have adverse effects on human health and the environment". this would mean that products resistant to already authorised antibiotics would remain. there are many less dangerous forms of genetic markers and eu policy is to prohibit any unnecessary use of antibiotics if there is a risk to human or animal health. the directive should take a clear position and insist on the immediate cessation of the use of these marker genes. the pharmaceutical industry wants to be totally exempted from this directive. the committee on the environment has adopted two contradictory amendments: the first in favour of a total exemption and the second reinforcing the line of the common position consisting of a conditional exemption with all the usual provisions on environmental risks. it is unacceptable that industry should be allowed to be exempted unless the provisions on environmental risks apply.
in writing. - today the liberal group supported the amendment tabled by jean louis bourlanges of the group of the european people's party, which called for the resolution to be separated from the explanatory statement. as an explanation, i will quote the following excerpts: 'hide the problems and forget them' seems to be the motto that has been passed on to the commission in certain areas. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. "the predominant influence which the administrative apparatus, too often dominated by groups linked by common interests, already exercised under president santer, is still increasing". these comments are in every way excessive, unfair and completely inappropriate in the context of the discharge procedure. the final vote on the resolution today gave us three options: to vote in favour, to vote against or to abstain. we have decided to abstain, which requires an explanation. voting for the stauner report is voting for the postponement of the discharge, which, in our view, should be granted now. as we have explained in the committee on budgetary control, we would have preferred to vote for the reform but against punishing the commission for the sins of its predecessors. the liberal group is logically in favour of many of the elements of the stauner report such as access to documents - the current commission has a duty to provide access to documents. but the political question remains: is this the right time or is this the right subject for parliament and the prodi commission to show their differences? we don't think he did. we are not voting for the report because we want to show that we prefer to tackle reform now rather than later because we think that so far this commission has shown that it is reforming. voting against the report would only have the effect of referring the report back to the committee on budgetary control without it being clearly determined what is to happen next and how. we are opposed to such an approach. we therefore decided to abstain. this is a reasoned abstention and not the result of any indifference. our group would have preferred to vote for discharge while encouraging reforms, which is also what the european commission is advocating. unfortunately, today's vote does not allow for such an option, so we have decided to abstain.

in writing. - this proposal for a directive covers cases where a road traffic accident occurs in the country of residence of the person causing the accident or in a third country. the aim of this directive is to ensure that the injured party has a right of direct action against the insurer of the person who caused the accident. let us remember that we live in the european union, where the principle of free movement of goods, persons, services and capital is in force. this directive, which rationalizes the question of civil liability in the event of an accident involving motor vehicles, is a logical extension of the directives put in place over the last 13 years to complete the european single market. it is only right and normal that in a system of complete freedom of movement of persons and of cross-border traffic, a rational and structured procedure should be in place for the injured parties to have recourse in the event of accidents. to this end, all insurance undertakings operating within the european union must appoint a representative in each member state who will be entitled to settle claims on behalf of the insurance undertaking in the event of an accident involving an eu national. the proposal also includes the creation of information centres to enable victims to identify the representative responsible for settling the claim. a compromise between the institutions was reached at a recent conciliation committee meeting in march. the most complex problem concerns the territorial application of the directive. that point is now resolved. the directive will apply to all accidents involving nationals of a member state of the union in the territory of the union or in any third country applying the green card system. the conciliation committee also accepted that member states may maintain and implement provisions which are more favourable to the victim than the provisions required to ensure compliance with this directive. i fully support this proposal and would like to commend mr rothley for the work he has done to bring these proposals to this level. the european union, as a result of the amsterdam treaty, has greater powers to produce legislation on consumer rights. this legislation governing the civil liability of motor vehicles and accidents in europe will ensure that the rights of 370 million european consumers in the event of accidents occurring in the european union are improved.
- mr president, i am pleased to be able to make a brief contribution to this debate and i congratulate the rapporteur on her excellent work. parliament has certainly spent a lot of time, on behalf of granted and is certainly not adequately rewarded by a reasonable income.
- mr president, first of all i would like to thank mrs roth-behrendt and of course mr böge for their commitment to this important issue. for some time now, the parliament, the commission and the member states have been seeking to introduce common eu-wide rules to tackle the problems of tse such as bse in cattle and scrapie in sheep. the basis of this proposal is that the discovery of a case of tse or the first appearance of bse in a member state would be covered by common regulations which would take effect immediately. until now, many of the actions to tackle bse have been undertaken on a unilateral basis by member states using national measures. the proposal on tse includes, among other things, the need to establish education programmes for farmers, veterinarians and civil servants; the removal of a list of specified risk material, an active surveillance programme using post-mortem analyses; and the strengthening of the ban on animal protein feedingstuffs for ruminants. the proposal also allows for the slaughter of animals and the payment of compensation to farmers for animals slaughtered. the principle of this approach cannot be called into question. common rules will increase the protection of animal health and, more importantly, they will ensure consumer protection by ensuring that the highest standards of disease prevention and eradication are in place in all member states and can be activated when necessary. these rules will provide a safeguard which can be followed in the event of any scientific information being produced or if more radical action is needed. however, i am aware, commissioner, that the majority of member states believe that the classification of countries for the purpose of eliminating mrs should be based on an internationally recognised code, drawn up and approved by the eu and the rest of the world - over 150 third countries - under the auspices of the oie, the world organisation for animal health, based in paris. the majority of member states are concerned about the approach taken by the scientific sub-groups and would prefer a more open and transparent classification system such as that established by the oie code, which is recognised and accepted internationally. this view was clearly expressed in the council working groups and was reflected in the final report on the presidency proposal, which was supported by the vast majority of member states. in order to reach agreement and implement this regulation quickly, and i hope to avoid a long conciliation procedure, the use of the common oie criteria is appropriate. after discussing this with mrs roth-behrendt, we tabled amendment no 51 to rule 5 as a compromise. i welcome the principle of establishing common regulations for the prevention and control of transmissible spongiform encephalopathies. however, this proposal may have negative consequences for sheep exporting countries and this is a major problem that is presently being raised. some feel that there should be separate rules for scrapie rather than the current mix of rules where the treatment of mrs in sheep depends on the incidence of bse in each country. in conclusion, i would like to reiterate my support for the use of the oie provisions as an internationally recognised basis for classification, a basis supported by 150 countries, in order to resolve trade disputes in the world, and this basis is recognised by the wto. i'm not giving you special treatment. the united kingdom would be in class 4, ireland in class 3 according to both the classification schemes of the css and the oie. the oie is meeting in paris next week to consider amendments to their classification system and i therefore hope to see further amendments to this regulation at second reading.
- mr president, i in turn congratulate all those involved in the resolution of this issue. rather than repeating what other colleagues have said, i think the crux of the problem is the training of young doctors. it is clear that although the nine-year time limit is not the perfect solution that parliament originally wanted, it is the best possible compromise. it is our duty to send a clear message that we expect all governments in the european union to comply fully with this nine-year period. while i know that there are rigorous tests and standards about whether or not to grant an extension, it should be granted only in the most exceptional circumstances and not in the name of a wavering ideology. furthermore, as mr hughes has indicated, the other rights are effective immediately. even if the overall reduction in working time has to be delayed for a while, the effective rest periods and other provisions take effect immediately. i will deal very briefly with two other points concerning the working time directive as a whole. firstly, as far as fishermen are concerned, the proposals put before us contain some attempts at compromise. however, legislation in this area is, as we know, very difficult to enforce because of external conditions, such as bad weather, which affect the work of fishermen. perhaps we should re-examine this at another level in the near future and try to come up with a better solution. secondly, transport workers: since we already have legislation on tachographs, why do we need to adopt a new separate directive on the problem of transport workers, particularly lorry drivers, etc.? perhaps it's time to revisit that point. new technologies - in particular digital tachographs and individual smart cards for each carrier to be inserted into the tachographs - could be the best way to regulate the hours drivers spend on the road and at work. this will also ensure that they receive benefits equivalent to those of other workers in the union. finally, i would like to congratulate mrs smet once again. i have another small request for the attention of the commissioner. perhaps it is time, after this conciliation and the presentation of this new legal text, to carry out an impact assessment of how the existing legislation works, whether it is beneficial to workers - whether it improves their health and safety - and whether it allows for job creation, which was one of the key arguments behind this text.
- it has never been easy to satisfy all the interested parties who lobbied and worked closely with the european institutions to bring forward amendments to the 1993 directive. that is the new directive, of course, and i am glad that it has been extended to offshore workers, fishermen and medical trainees. the most controversial part of the directive was related to the doctors in training who would benefit from this text. it should be recalled that the council had originally proposed an exemption for a period of 13 years before doctors in training could benefit from the benefits of this directive. naturally, parliament has put forward its own proposals to support the right of doctors in training to be covered by this directive after a period of 4 years. hence the conciliation procedure in which you are involved. but as sweet as the tone of the procedure's conclusions may be, the bottom line is that the trainee doctors will not benefit for at least nine years. according to the conciliation committee's conclusions, doctors in training will benefit from the benefits after a transitional period of five years and a transposition period of four years. i fully support the right of medical trainees to reap the benefits of this directive, but i will reluctantly accept the conciliation committee's conclusions. in conclusion, i would like to make a very important point, namely that in exceptional circumstances an extension of two years is possible and that extension can actually be extended to three years if the governments declare that the reduction would cause real administrative problems. i hope that this directive will be implemented soon.
- mr president, this proposal will put seven million additional workers in europe under the protection of the working time directive, allowing them to have reasonable working conditions and adequate rest periods. however, to enjoy a 48-hour workweek, trainee doctors must wait at least nine years and perhaps 12 years. i'm not going to pretend to be happy about this. but this is the best we can do if we are to put an end, once and for all, to the council's delaying tactics. it has been clear since 1993 that groups excluded from the directive at the time would one day be protected by it. some may therefore ask why, during these seven years, almost nothing has been done in ireland or britain to increase the number of doctors so that the directive can be implemented. in ireland, some doctors in training work more than 100 hours a week, with the average being 70 to 80. it was only last night that they lifted the strike notice, as they had reached an interim agreement that would ensure they were actually paid for the overtime. in some cases they were paid nothing at all, in others they were paid only half the normal rate. some progress has been made and the conclusion of the conciliation has undoubtedly contributed to this. the british and irish governments have been fighting tooth and nail within the council to slow down the process. it was unfortunate. the work schedule of medical students resembles a 19th-century horror story, such as one found in dickens' novels. i find it strange that at the beginning of the 21st century we are discussing, against all common sense, health and safety. i call on all national governments, including ireland, not to use the additional conditions to delay the implementation of this directive beyond the nine-year period, and i would remind them that this extension can only be an exceptional measure applied if all other measures have already been taken.

in writing. - according to the world health organization, the definition of the term "reproductive health" includes the term "regulation of fertility" including the notion of "interruption of unwanted pregnancies", in other words, abortion on demand. in the interests of subsidiarity, paragraphs and amendments relating to reproductive health should not be included in a european parliament document unless they are clearly and honestly defined. there is a generally accepted terminology throughout the world - 'primary health care' - defined at the who conference in alma ata in 1978 and which refers to the health of women and mothers without including abortion on demand. in addition, in paragraph 18, the use of language renders the meaning ambiguous and leaves room for interpretation; the prevention of 'girl pregnancies' unfortunately can also mean that we are particularly concerned with preventing pregnancies when the child conceived is a girl. ambiguities like this should not be in a report designed to serve as instructions to the negotiating team at the new york conference for the remainder of the beijing+5 session. with regard to paragraph 26, which specifies that 'respect for women's rights should be a condition for the granting of aid and should be closely monitored in all external relations and cooperation', the european union should not link specifications to the granting of aid or impose conditions for the receipt of aid by women, understanding that women are extremely vulnerable to the use of coercion and coercive measures in order to respect their fundamental human rights such as housing, food, education and basic health care. the european union must exercise extreme caution to ensure that the current definitions are compatible with the universal declaration of human rights, since women are not a minority group.
