- i would really like to support this report. i think it is extremely important and i think it is essential to support nafo's efforts to put an end to the problematic activities of non-contracting parties. in practical terms, 'non-contracting party vessels' are often flagged as flags of convenience or flags of free registration fleet. these fleets belong to countries which grant shipowners the right to fly their flag in return for a fee. under international law, it is the responsibility of the state concerned to ensure that the shipowner complies with the relevant rules and regulations, including conservation, safety and labour standards, etc. but, and this is a serious problem, it turns out that these countries have no intention of enforcing the rules and regulations in force and this is the main reason why they are using these unscrupulous shipowners who can do whatever they want. they can break the law with impunity. all the shipowner has to do is buy the flag of the country, which will allow him to fish with impunity. it is essential to put an end to these practices. the list of such countries is very long, but belize, the republic of panama, honduras, and the island of saint vincent and the grenadines are the most common. many of the vessels in question are active in the nafo area, but they are also active elsewhere. they are very active in the tuna fishery in the atlantic ocean, in the ices zone. more than any other regional organisation, iccat has been committed to combating fishing activities on board vessels flying the flag of convenience. more spectacularly - and i urge others to follow suit - iccat has just banned imports of bluefin tuna from one of its signatory members, equatorial guinea. cicta is really doing a great job and others would do well to follow its example. the problem cannot be solved by organizations such as iccat and nafo alone. the responsibilities are many and must be taken seriously. many shipowners like to fly their flags as a way of making a living. there's no way to enforce the law. by selling their flag, the countries concerned allow the shipowners to fly their flag; they pretend they don't see anything and don't care. this explains the success of these practices. the eu is the one with the big pot. according to lloyds maritime services, 1 323 vessels over 24 metres were flying the flag of convenience in december 1999, 168 of which were owned by companies based in eu member states. with 116 ships, and no one will be surprised, spain holds the record, but it is not alone. each country seems to have its own preferences. for example, french ships fly the flags of réunion, saint vincent and the seychelles. greece is turning to cyprus and panama. the dutch prefer belize and st. vincent and, of course, four ships from my home country fly the belize flag. the flags of panama and belize are the most sought after by the british. the presence of belize is not surprising, given last year's revelations about the business relationship that michael ashcroft, the conservative party treasurer, has with the country. the spanish fleet has trade with many countries, including belize, honduras, the netherlands antilles, the republic of panama, and many others. the only country that surpasses spain is taiwan, with 117 flags of convenience. last year the commission tried to react: it proposed to freeze subsidies for the transfer of ownership of ships to countries flying free-of-charge flags. the council rejected this proposal. he prefers to use public money to fund ways to break the law, and that's not acceptable. there is at least one organization that is trying to alert public opinion. two weeks ago, greenpeace sent a ship out into the middle of the south atlantic, and the crew found a multitude of tuna boats flying the flag of complacency. he also discovered a japanese-owned transport vessel flying the flag of panama, both signatories to the iccat, receiving fishery products from non-signatory vessels from belize. this is a practice that is totally against the rules of the iccat, and i really think we need to address this issue. the european union must take responsibility for this issue.
- mr president, i would like to congratulate mr varela suanzes-carpegna on the considerable amount of work he has done and is doing on all the issues raised by the committee on fisheries. the question of the means we use to protect marine life outside our territorial waters is not popular. it is not a matter of much concern to public opinion in the european union. the european union's efforts in this direction are becoming more and more well understood, but i would say that since the common fisheries policy has been in existence, and this is not new, the union has largely failed to convince european public opinion of the importance of the role it has played in trying to improve the situation of marine life, to provide the public with scientific information and to take the necessary measures. often, member states and national governments do not give us the support we need to convince the public of the determination and persistence needed to enforce all laws and regulations. despite these efforts, some 80% of all important stocks are still overexploited and continue to deplete. the measures we are dealing with may be those we are best able to manage, but they seem rather weak and barely sufficient to achieve our objective. the community market for seafood is the largest and most profitable in the world. we must be prepared to enforce and impose all necessary trade sanctions on all states and all products associated with this illegal trade, whether by any means. this is not just about protecting our interests, because settlements beyond our 200-mile limit can be affected by our activities. similarly, settlements within our borders can be affected by outside activities. and then there is the whole question of our duty to protect these resources, which are a good of humanity. i believe that in today's world, people who can afford to buy large ships and equip them do not impose any limits on their ability to exploit marine resources. i think that no individual should have the right, simply because he has the financial means and the technical capacity and lives in a region conducive to this kind of activity, to exploit these resources and to enrich himself, as sometimes happens. we may be at a point where these resources should be tendered and paid for and the public could get some compensation from the very rich people who get very rich very quickly - this is already happening in new zealand. the european union must take into account, in the context of the revision of its common fisheries policy, the fact that marine resources are a public good and that a certain number of people believe they have the right to exploit them and to derive wealth from them. the public would be broadly supportive of the introduction of any compensation system. in the past, people would risk their lives on poorly equipped ships. today, fishermen have the technical ability to spot shoals of fish and exploit them, and they should pay a fee for doing so.
- mr president, today's topic is important. this is a very technical subject and difficult for the public to understand. gradual improvement of the regulations will contribute, in the long term, to the good health of the marine environment for future generations. we can use our resources in a positive way. the union's fisheries sector is not self-sufficient. it only covers 50% of our needs. good conservation measures and better practices would improve the living standards of more people who live off fishing. with a little time and care, the value of fish landed in the european union could increase by some €4 billion. it's a highly technical subject. it is important that we do research, that we continue to review the regulations and that we gradually convince the public that the european commission is not a hostile and distant organisation sent to persecute fishermen and prevent them from earning a living. we must avoid using occasions like this to make inflammatory statements about the need to reduce the size of fleets on the grounds of declining stocks, the high price of fish due to its scarcity in the european union and the interest in this foodstuff for its nutritional qualities. we're pushing to catch more and more fish. there are complaints about the multiannual guidance programme and its alleged uselessness. surely a transport company will refuse to run ten buses when one bus is enough to carry passengers. it is the same principle for trains and lorries. so why are fishing communities pushing to maintain large, expensive fleets when in reality there is not enough fish and they are held by quotas? the principle of quotas means that fishermen and fishing communities have an interest in keeping a limited number of vessels in the water. such an attitude is more favourable to national and regional economic planning. do not be fooled into thinking that we want to keep ships and that this will help in some way to keep jobs. we have had headlines in the newspapers, the mere publication of a report in which the commission states that certain fleets will have to be reduced. these reports were circulated in scotland and ireland, and they mentioned massive job cuts. it doesn't have to come to that. let us sit down, let us come to the fisheries committee, let us listen to the officials, let us listen to the researchers and let us try to find a sensible solution to these problems instead of maintaining an atmosphere of hostility. ireland broadcasts a programme on the sea. the show is called "seascapes". on the eve of the maastricht referendum, the channel's executives have hardened the tone of their anti-european show, which is broadcast on a loop in order to win the good graces of fishing communities who feel that, somehow, the european commission is there to go against their interests. let us try, as responsible public representatives, to tell our electorate the truth, rather than mislead, provoke and frighten them.

- mr president, i am glad that there is a general consensus among the 15 member states that the legislation on the sale, presentation and advertising of tobacco products needs to be fundamentally changed. the protection of the health of european citizens must be at the heart of the priorities of all european legislators at all times. as a result of the amsterdam treaty, we now have as much say in public health as the governments of the eu member states. let's use this advantage wisely. the problem of smoking and the damage that it causes to the health of european citizens is a very serious public health issue. i support most of the key amendments adopted last month by the committee on the environment calling for warnings on cigarette packs to be larger than those proposed by the commission. warnings on the dangers of smoking to public health should cover 40% of the front and 60% of the back of each pack sold in the european union. labelling requirements should also generally be more stringent and include more comprehensive health warnings and the list of ingredients should also be available on request. descriptions such as low tar, mild and light should be banned in future. i am in favour of removing such descriptions because they give the impression that these cigarettes are less harmful to health than other cigarettes: this is clearly misleading. the tobacco industry must be prohibited from using such misleading advertising. the environment committee also asked that the warnings be printed in black on a white background to give them more impact. the committee also considers that general warnings such as that tobacco kills are inadequate and i agree with this analysis. messages such as 'smoking kills half a million people in the european union every year' or '85% of lung cancer is caused by smoking' should be printed on cigarette packets in the future.
- mr president, the european union spends one billion euros a year on subsidies for tobacco production - one billion euros. tobacco kills more than 500 000 european citizens - half a million of our citizens. it would be cheaper to go out and shoot them. we'd save on health care costs. how many billions of euros are spent in health care services to save the lives of people who try to kill themselves with cigarettes? as you may have gathered, i am a non-smoking, intolerant person. i am interested, and i am extremely concerned, to see the increasing number of people, especially young girls, who are starting to smoke. i am not talking about people my age or older who have actually smoked all their lives, but the message is not getting through to young people. it is certainly not an impossible task for the best minds in the political, medical, scientific and business worlds to devise a good message so that our young people do not start smoking. that's what i'm asking. can 't we get the message to them ? the general health care systems, in ireland it is our social security, should fund those who want to quit smoking and provide free prescriptions for nicotine replacement therapies. the state should finance this.
the rapporteur. - mr president, marine pollution, both accidental and intentional, is a major threat to the marine environment in western europe. this region is one of the most industrialized areas in the world, where chemicals, radioactive substances and hydrocarbons are daily dumped into the marine environment and transported by cargo ships. the region also has one of the highest concentrations of maritime transport and several of the busiest shipping routes in the world. there are several tragic examples of accidental pollution by oil tankers and other ships carrying other types of dangerous cargo. the recent example of the erika shows what can happen from an environmental, human and economic point of view. this is a disaster. more fortunately than thanks to good organization, there has not yet been a major accident involving a cargo ship carrying radioactive substances that would have spilled a large load. however, it must be said that there have been many incidents. in 1973, a source of cobalt-60 was lost in the north sea and has never been found. in 1984, mount louis sank into the english channel with 375 tons of uranium hexafluoride on its side. as regards discharges into the sea, in 1983 sellafield discharged highly radioactive waste into the marine environment. in 1985, there was a hinkley point leak. in 1986, 135 cubic metres of radioactive water were dumped into the wylfa sea. the commission's refusal to take its responsibilities and to include radioactive substances explicitly is unacceptable and it will have to reconsider its position on this subject. historically, the marine environment is used as the ultimate dumping ground for most of our industrial waste. although industrial and radioactive waste continues to emanate from various sources, the practice of direct dumping, with little regard for the future, is now widely considered unacceptable. however, the legacy of the years when substances were simply dipped remains and sometimes haunts us, with serious consequences. it's a lesson that we can't just throw things away: it's impossible, because they're always there in one way or another. this report recognises the need for cooperation in the field of accidental marine pollution. in addition, the definition of accidental marine pollution should be broadened to include radioactive, historical and operational substances and other harmful substances. although more attention is being paid, and rightly so, the impact of accidents in terms of chronic pollution, operational discharges and spills of oil, radioactive materials and other harmful substances is a cause for great concern because they go largely unnoticed. from an environmental point of view, the question of whether a substance is released accidentally or intentionally is irrelevant. moreover, although direct dumping of industrial substances, including the military dumping of surplus ammunition, is largely abandoned by member states, the sites of such dumping continue to pose a real threat to fisheries and, in some cases, to the safety of human beings. often the lives of fishermen are at risk, as is the health and well-being of people visiting coastal areas or living near the sea. furthermore, as the sea is the ultimate reservoir of industrial materials coming from the coast or rivers, these sources should be included in the definition. the transboundary nature of the marine environment, which means that pollution in one area can have an impact on a wider region, means that a community framework for cooperation on accidental marine pollution is needed. sharing of information related to oil, radioactive substances and other harmful substances transported by sea, discharged into the sea or discharged under historical discharge systems is a prerequisite for facilitating the preparation and coordination of any response necessary to mitigate the impact of pollution incidents on the environment and human health. this requires a rapid response: the longer it takes to control an incident, the greater the impact. it is too late to try to get information after a serious pollution problem has occurred. knowledge of this type of potential pollution and its extent is also a prerequisite for being properly prepared, in the appropriate places, from the technological point of view, with personnel and equipment. the risks to the public and the environment must also be clearly identified and the justified public concerns mitigated through a precise risk assessment. public confidence in the authorities is important to ensure an adequate response. in addition, the public has a right to know what preparations have been made, what types of substances pose threats and what impacts can be expected. to this end, the publication of information is a vital component as it establishes a means by which the public can access information, such as emergency telephone numbers and contact numbers. such information can easily be made available and updated on the internet and through permanent channels. finally, once the marine environment has been contaminated or polluted, it is impossible to completely clean up and repair the damage. erika is a good example. however, a rapid, effective and adequate response, coordinated across national borders, is needed to mitigate the impact on the environment and public health in the event of pollution. this is part of the information that must be shared by all parties.
- mr president, as the various speakers have already pointed out, when the erika sank last year, it spilled a slab of oil into the sea along the french atlantic coast. the floodplain has threatened wildlife, local tourism and the fishing industry. however, and this is regrettable, it was a disaster to add to the list of disasters for the marine environment, such as that of the braer off the shetland islands and the sea empress off wales. recently in ireland, we were fortunate enough to escape a similar disaster when an oil slick, 88 miles off the isle of arran, threatened to engulf the irish coast, but fortunately, the danger passed. an additional difficulty in combating marine pollution is that, due to the nature of the sea, it can quickly reach the coast of a large number of member states at once. this is why an effective and coordinated european response is needed rather than leaving the problem to the first member state to be affected. i welcome the report by mrs mckenna. i'd like to pay tribute to him for his work. but the real question is how we will implement this legislation and what kind of actions we will take. the ability to respond quickly, with sufficient resources, is an essential element of any strategy that must be put in place from the beginning of a disaster to minimise environmental damage. it is doubtful whether this can be adequately ensured at national level, and therefore these measures must be taken at european level. the most effective way to achieve this is to establish a european coastguard service which, in addition to being responsible for pollution control, could also be responsible for surveillance on the high seas, enforcing the common fisheries policy and also assisting in rescues at sea. this is a question which i think should be discussed at the next igc.
- mr president, there is an important meeting of the ospar committee on 26 june in copenhagen. the main objective of the ospar convention is to bring marine pollution back to near-zero levels and historical levels by the year 2020. from the irish point of view, we know that marine pollution in the irish sea has been increasing in recent years and this is a direct consequence of the authorization granted in december 1993 to the thorp nuclear waste reprocessing plant. studies in the irish sea have clearly shown that levels of a specific nuclear substance, technetium-99, are 15 times higher than they were seven years ago. the uk government has so far only outlined the strategy it intends to implement to comply with the terms of the ospar convention. it is simply not enough and the irish and the europeans have a right to know the true answers of the british government to many questions. for years the british government has tried to get rid of many irish politicians and environmental groups by reassuring them about the sellafield and thorp nuclear plants. it cannot continue to do so because of this year's alarming revelations about the safety and quality control standards that are totally inadequate in the operation of these plants. the irish government presented a draft decision for the ospar meeting, inviting the contracting parties to agree to an acceleration in the implementation of the ospar strategy. this strategy includes the cessation of nuclear reprocessing and associated activities at the sellafield and thorp plants. this will play a key role in reducing marine pollution in the irish sea. if the uk government is to comply with its legal obligations under the ospar convention, it should declare in copenhagen on 26 june that it will present a timetable for the closure of sellafield and thorp. i would like to congratulate mrs mckenna on her excellent report.

in writing. -(en) we are in favour of the major changes that will occur in european culture as a result of digital technology. however, good public service broadcasting is still essential. ensuring access to public services and content of general interest will be a fundamental guarantee of universality. it will also be an important driver of digital success by increasing its appeal. the universal availability of the public service requires ensuring that, whatever digital system people use, they have guaranteed access to the information that is essential to a well-informed society. considerations of general interest suggest that such content should be accessible through all major communication platforms, irrespective of the access regime ultimately adopted. 'mandatory access' rules will be necessary to ensure the universal availability of public service content on other major distribution systems, and on the memory of receivers where it forms a significant gateway. the 'importance' rules will ensure that the content and channels of the public service can be easily found in the electronic programme guides. digital television is the only converged technology without basic interoperability. regulatory measures will be needed to promote interoperability between networks and the tools that will be used to access digital television and interactive services. interoperability can be achieved through the declaration and licensing of the key standards, interfaces and creation tools needed to reach the end user. this will promote interoperability without hampering innovation. third parties will need to have access to this information at the same time as the owners of the bridge have access to their own services. it is important to strike the right balance between the level of the european union and that of the individual member states. it is true that the regulatory framework for infrastructure should be established at european level and implemented at national level. national legislatures will need the freedom to apply the infrastructure regulation in accordance with their national situation, but they will need to be prepared to justify themselves at european level. in such a case, the member state should be free to set its own priorities. as we are already in a world of television without frontiers, it is important in the case of ireland that public service broadcasting, whether in the north or the south, recognise all the cultural divisions that have led to mutual respect in the good friday agreement, which carries a strong democratic mandate.

- mr president, the process of establishing a common european security and defence policy has made considerable progress. the european councils of cologne, helsinki and lisbon have all made important contributions in this respect. but let us be honest, the decisions taken at these summits and those which may be taken at the feira summit will remain wishful thinking until the european union has the means to implement them. i agree with the recent comments made by commissioner patten, when he criticised member state governments for making loud statements without giving themselves the means and staff to put them into practice. if the member states are really serious about establishing a genuine security and defence policy, they must be able to deliver on their promises. but we must be realistic about what we can achieve. but at the moment we cannot speak of an autonomous european task force. this autonomy must always be seen in the light of our dependence on nato. it is nato that still holds the key military equipment and it is nato that has enabled eu governments to deploy their troops in bosnia and herzegovina and kosovo. until we have intelligence capabilities equivalent to those of the us and transport capabilities to rapidly deploy eu military personnel to conflict-affected areas, we cannot do without cooperation with nato and a structured eu-nato dialogue that is not a mere extension of the euo-nato acquis. these realities must temper our discussions at the feira summit and at our other summits, as well as the need to keep in mind the status and role of the united nations. i have heard that the feira council may endorse the concept of deploying a rapid reaction police force after the military interventions have been completed and i welcome this news. the experience in kosovo has shown the need for such an initiative and i welcome the fact that this will perhaps be done at the feira council. that's my conclusion.
in writing. - it was important that the tobacco industry was not able to prevent today's vote in parliament. more than half a million people were victims of tobacco in the eu in 1990. despite the enormous pressure exerted by the tobacco industry, including the attempt to declare the procedure illegal, the european parliament will continue to defend public health with firmness. i welcome the commitment to clarify the labelling of cigarette packs. health warnings shall cover 40% of the front and 50% of the back of the package. the industry must be obliged to provide details of the ingredients of cigarettes, including the type of tobacco used, and equally importantly, the composition of the filter and the additives used in their manufacture. each brand should list all additives, not just the prohibited ingredients. this must be made public and not be hidden behind some mysterious 'trade secret'. people have a right to accurate information about the health risks of tobacco. environmentalists want to ban most of the more than 600 additives in cigarettes. we need to know what these substances are and make sure they are properly tested. smokers have a right to know exactly what is in the cigarettes they smoke.
in writing. - i welcome the adoption of this report, as it guarantees the right to a high level of public health protection and could pave the way for the eventual long-term eradication of smoking in the eu. if we take the example of the community approach to food safety legislation which is based on the principle that consumers have a right to know what they are consuming, the same should be true of tobacco products: smokers should have the right to know exactly what they are smoking and what the effects of tobacco are. we as legislators share with the manufacturers the responsibility to ensure that the public is fully informed of the dangers of smoking. recommendations on the front of packaging are welcome, as they dispel the myth that these products are less harmful to a person's health.
counsel. - (pt) mr president, as you know, it is the commission, in consultation with a committee of representatives of the member states, which conducts the negotiations within the ospar committee on matters of community competence. if, at the end of these negotiations, the parties to the ospar convention adopt a decision which, in the opinion of the commission, should be incorporated into the community legal system, it is then for the commission to formulate a proposal for a council decision to the effect that the community adopts the ospar decision. the council will examine this proposal with due care. in any event, the proposal referred to by the honourable member has not yet been submitted to the council, which is therefore not in a position to take a decision on the matter.
- i know that the portuguese presidency is well aware of this issue. as you know, the ospar commission met in sintra two years ago and made some progress on pollution of the marine environment. my question is more specifically about radioactive emissions. i know that france is part of the troika and that it will take over the presidency in july. france has exerted very strong pressure and is one of the signatory states of the agreement on the reduction of radioactive emissions. however, a recent greenpeace study revealed the presence of high levels of radioactive emissions in the cape hague area. is the council aware of this study? would he like any more information about himself ? does he intend to draw the attention of the french presidency to this study? on the one hand, the authorities claim to control marine radioactive pollution, but on the other hand, we have evidence that radioactive pollution exists. we are faced with a contradiction. the council must act. i understand the purpose of the commission's proposals and look forward to discussing them in more detail with the commission when we have the copenhagen conclusions.
counsel. - (pt) mr president, the decision of the member states of the european union to develop their military capabilities by 2003 by having a capacity of 50 000 to 60 000 men, in what is known as the headline goal, was taken in helsinki. this decision reaffirms that the european union's crisis management action will be conducted in accordance with the principles of the united nations charter and the objectives of the osce european security charter. in addition, the declaration of helsinki, which was endorsed by the heads of state and government of the fifteen, expressly states that the european union recognises the primacy of the united nations security council for the maintenance of international peace and security. the recognition of the need to respect the principles of the un charter and the principles of the european security charter is so evident that the portuguese presidency stresses in the report it will present to the feira european council next tuesday that the european union must establish close cooperation with the united nations, the osce and the council of europe in the field of crisis management and conflict prevention. no un security council resolution is necessary to give overall and general legitimacy to the european union's action. the union shall commit itself politically and at the highest level to respecting the primacy of the security council; the union thus undertakes, in the presence of a given operation, to respect the primacy of that body and, therefore, the resolutions adopted therein on a case-by-case basis. however, it should be borne in mind that some of the petersberg-type missions may be carried out without the authorisation or the 'green light' of the united nations security council. the petersberg missions cover, to use the exact terms envisaged, 'humanitarian missions and the evacuation of nationals, peacekeeping missions and combat forces missions for crisis management, including peacekeeping operations'. thus, only for the latter, for which the use of military force is required, is a un security council resolution deemed necessary. it seems clear that the execution of actions, particularly those of a humanitarian nature, is not without legitimacy on the part of the united nations security council, which does not mean, however, that the principles of the united nations charter must not be respected in the execution of this type of mission.
- (pt) mr president, the petersberg missions have different dimensions. some missions are purely humanitarian, such as evacuation missions. i wonder if you think that a decision of the un security council is necessary to carry out an evacuation mission in the face of a serious humanitarian situation. i have also made it clear that anything which involves the use of military force, that is to say, peacekeeping missions, naturally involves the use of and subordination to the security council. otherwise, we are simply fulfilling the obligations of the european union in the framework of the united nations, especially in view of the fact that some countries are members of the united nations security council. therefore, the spirit of the un charter will of course be respected in the type of activities that the european union will be called upon to carry out in the framework of the petersberg tasks that do not involve the use of military force.

charter of fundamental rights has been raised here and i would like to emphasise that the european union has adopted a methodology for the drafting of this charter. it is a convention which is itself in the process of being worked on, and has not yet concluded its work. it will have to conclude and decide on many things, which it has not yet decided because it is working on: whether the charter is only a declaration of principles or whether it is integrated into the treaties, whether the charter will be one of the first examples of enhanced cooperation or not, how it applies to citizens, whether it applies only to european members of the european union or to other european residents of the countries of the european union, or to foreigners and even illegal immigrants - this is a problem that has not yet been answered - how it is integrated with the european convention on human rights, how it is integrated with the national courts and with the existing european courts or union. no body of the european union has deliberated, although we are all of course countries of the european union. the portuguese presidency has always been careful to make this distinction, stating that this was not a matter for the european union. why would you do that ? on the one hand, to safeguard the normal functioning of the european union and the achievement of the european union agenda, and on the other hand, to avoid creating any obstacles to austria's full participation in the life of the european union and its bodies. whenever any weakness in this principled process was pointed - mr president, we welcome the commission's proposal to improve the control system and i would like to thank mr casaca for his report. this is a very technical subject, requiring an understanding of almost all the details of the whole of the common agricultural policy in order to be able to understand it. mr casaca made a considerable effort to explain this to parliament. i would also like to thank the commission for its repeated efforts to reorganize the whole system of accounting in the field of agriculture. this is extremely important. we have recently spoken of the regional funds and the cohesion funds, but the common agricultural policy is vital. it is the most important instrument we have for cohesion, since it is also the only instrument the community has for rural policy. it brings immense benefits to peripheral regions and to people engaged in activities which would otherwise be unsustainable. the policy in question had been given an inadequate name in the past, when most of the funds were directed to the agricultural industry through processors and exporters. it allowed fraud and racketeering on a large scale by the payment of monetary compensatory amounts. the reorganisation of this policy puts us in a new situation. it is worth noting that the most recent report issued by the court of auditors for 1998 did not draw attention to any major errors by the commission in this sector as a whole. some problems have arisen, but they were isolated, of low gravity and related to aid sectors, bonuses and an inability of national governments to understand european legislation. we must acknowledge that overall the commission received a good health bulletin for 1998, which is the most recent report available to us. the commission is to be congratulated on this. given that this sector represents about 48% of the total budget - €38 or €39 billion - spread over fifteen countries, it is a feat that the budget in this area does not exceed the margin of error of 2.5% and that the waste and the risk of loss are below the average for the whole budget. changes to the whole system have made it easier to control. the fact that more money is now going directly to farmers helps to understand exactly what is happening. the proposed amendments will adapt our accounting system to the new policies that have been designed. it is reasonable to expect member states to produce the kind of information needed to ensure that the european commission has accurate data, both from a management and accounting point of view, on where and who receives money directly, down to the last beneficiary. that's a good thing. as regards the amendments proposed by mr casaca, we all accept them. we have received complaints from some people about the proposal to introduce tobacco. since, as things stand, money goes to the processors, it is somewhat reasonable to say that there should be no extension. but we spend a billion euros on the tobacco diet. it concerns about 135 000 farmers and 135 000 hectares, which means approximately 7 600 euro per hectare and 7 600 euro per farmer. that's a lot of money. it is therefore only right that we should ensure that this money reaches farmers and is applied to hectares, as mr casaca has suggested. my group is calling for a separate vote to allow those who do not like the tobacco regime to vote against. on the other hand, our amendment is a message to the commission that we really think this should be clarified so that the money goes directly to where it is supposed to go: to rural areas and farmers.

- mr president, i would like to thank my colleague and friend mrs stauner for her report. we sometimes seem to forget that the subject on the agenda is the discharge for 1998. i have read with great attention the court of auditors' report on the accounts for this year, particularly those relating to agriculture and the structural funds. i have come to the conclusion from my extensive experience of this parliament and european affairs that things have been getting better and better and that 1998 was one of the best years we have ever had. the problems that arose - a possible error of 4% in the field of agriculture and a little more in that of the structural funds - were not due to the fact that the commission had not spent the corresponding funds efficiently, but to a lack - and this was a small failure - of control by the member states and european governments to ensure that they and their administrations did their job the way they were supposed to do it. that's my first point. the second concerns the fléchard case. i do not think it is reasonable to tell the european citizens that this case has cost the community budget 14 million euros. when you present things like that, you use a kind of accounting jargon that prevents the average citizen from understanding what really happened. the truth is that in this case the fléchard company was suspected of fraud and the irish government punished it by imposing a fine of 17 million euros. fléchard then brought an action against the irish government and the court issued an order preventing the irish government from seizing the €17 million. it was at this stage that the commission stepped in to propose a regulation. the worst thing that can be said about this regulation is that it was founded on a legal basis which was not in force when the fraud was committed, but which came into force before the case was closed. i think it is a great exaggeration to equate this with a loss of 14 million euros. in conclusion, i would say that the lesson to be drawn from this case is that in future this parliament must cooperate with the european commission so that national governments do not interfere, as in the past, in its powers and also so that the commission can count on our support when it goes after a government.
- madam president, i would like, on behalf of my group, to warmly welcome mr chirac, both as president-in-office of the council and as french president who has come here to testify to the importance that france and he personally attach to the work of this presidency.
- mr president, the european life programme is a key initiative of the european union to integrate environmental measures into the general framework of community policies. the european union, as a political entity, has a moral and legal obligation under article 130 of the maastricht treaty to ensure the protection and promotion of a clean environment. in fact, between 1992 and 1999, the life programme helped to co-finance almost 1 600 projects for a total community contribution of around eur 2.1 billion. i am particularly pleased that the european life programme has helped to promote the use of clean technologies and has helped to finance many waste recycling projects. as far as ireland is concerned, over 90% of our waste is still treated by landfilling. i therefore welcome the initiatives taken by the irish government, in particular those to divert 50% of all household waste to landfills, to reduce the volume of biodegradable waste destined for landfill by 65%, to build more waste recovery infrastructure and to reduce methane emissions by up to 80%. i also welcome the fact that member states have allocated an additional eur 27 million to the new life programme for the next five years, bringing the total budget to eur 640 million. i congratulate the rapporteur.

- mr president, i understand the principle expressed in this report of completing the internal market in rail transport, thus allowing rail freight to compete with road freight. this is necessary for the long-term survival of the european railway sector and all member states should support this objective. we now need to agree on how to achieve this, bearing in mind the difficulties it implies for certain member states. i speak for my country, ireland. the republic of ireland and northern ireland both seek to extend the derogation granted to them in december 1999 for geographical and geopolitical reasons. ireland is a small country isolated from the rest of europe. railway operators from the rest of the european union do not have easy access to the irish market. i have seen firsthand the continuous attacks on the north-south railway lines during the past thirty years of political turmoil. the opportunity to develop and prosper is opening up for them for the first time. the implementation of the railway liberalisation package today will require ireland to develop a complex administrative structure, but it will not derive the same benefits as other member states. i am sure that the irish government has subscribed to the general principle of the separation of infrastructure and operation and has committed itself to pursuing the restructuring of the irish railways to that end. i ask that the current derogation be maintained in order to allow ireland time to make these changes, bearing in mind that, should an eu rail operator wish to enter the irish market, the derogation would end immediately. finally, while i support this derogation, i personally hope that it will not in any way delay the much needed improvement and extension of the irish railway system.
- mr president, i would like to raise an issue which should be of great concern to this house, and in particular to the members of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and the committee on budgetary control. my own country is in serious breach of community law and the statutes of the european investment bank. under community law, member states are not allowed to propose or nominate their own nationals directly to the board of governors for the post of vice-president of the eib. this is a clear violation of the statutes of the european investment bank. only the board of directors of the eib, which is a separate body, has the exclusive right to make appointments. in ireland, the situation is contrary to this principle. the irish government, in breach of the law, appointed someone who, despite having no experience in economic and financial affairs, happens to be a judge who was disqualified by the supreme court and forced to resign before being impeached by the irish parliament. the case is currently before the irish courts. this is a matter of great importance to this parliament, which is concerned because the european union allocates guarantee funds to the eib. parliament must take note of this, because if we allow member states to break the law in this way, it will have serious implications for the european union as a whole. i would ask the president whether he could instruct the committee on economic and monetary affairs and the committee on budgetary control to look into this matter because it needs to be dealt with very quickly. this could set a very bad precedent for other member states and for appointments to this body.

- madam president, it is appropriate to reflect for a moment on how this interinstitutional agreement came to be before us today. it is very important that all the european institutions act effectively and efficiently, and the 370 million european citizens must have support and confidence in the union's internal legislative process. the european commission is one of the key legislative bodies, with the power of initiative for the enactment of directives and regulations. the european council represents the interests of the 15 governments of the union and has a very large legislative power. parliament is a democratically elected legislative body whose main responsibility is to scrutinise the other key institutions. if the european union is to act more effectively, then it is very important that the relationship between the commission and parliament is well defined. that is why the framework agreement between the commission and parliament is being put in place. the negotiations for this framework agreement were tortuous. this document may not be perfect, but it is certainly a step in the right direction. it is more binding than the agreement which has existed between the two institutions for the last five years. i would now like to touch on some of the key provisions of this agreement. the two institutions will now agree to extend political dialogue and cooperation, improve the flow of information and consult and inform parliament on administrative reform within the commission. there is also agreement, although not very extensive, on the legislative process of the union. international agreements and enlargement negotiations and procedures are in place for the transmission of confidential commission documents and information. it could be argued that most of this framework agreement is simply clarifying existing provisions in line with the current eu treaties. i know, for example, that the commission must keep parliament informed of the enlargement negotiations so that it can express its views in due course, through the appropriate parliamentary procedures. this is a fair and appropriate procedure, since the european parliament must approve the right of any new state to become a full member of the union. parliament also has the final say in the approval process of international agreements such as wto agreements. parliament should not be expected to take important decisions on such matters unless the necessary and relevant information has been given to members. the commission must therefore initiate open procedures to provide up-to-date information on all the international regulations, directives and agreements discussed and promulgated by the union. in conclusion, i am convinced that there is still a need to clarify the procedures for transmitting confidential information.
in writing. - the rail infrastructure package adopted by the transport council last december was the result of difficult negotiations. the common position represents a delicate balance which supports the principle of liberalisation of the sector while taking due account of the particularities of each member state. part of this common position includes a complex derogation for ireland, northern ireland and greece, as well as additional considerations for luxembourg. in the case of greece and the two parts of ireland, there are clear geographical barriers to their full participation in a single railway market. in this context, the full implementation of the rail infrastructure package would be a considerable burden for the administration of those member states without the benefits for consumers provided for in the package. the amendments i proposed to the reports by mr jarzembowski and mr swoboda were intended to restore the balance established by the common position. the council's position is in my view in the interests of both the railway sector and consumers. i would also like to stress that the derogation for ireland, northern ireland, greece and luxembourg covers only those elements of the package where implementation serves no real practical purpose. in addition, the safeguard clauses embedded in the derogation itself could cause the derogation to be terminated as a whole, should a new player appear on the irish or greek railway market. in conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, as a member of the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism, i recognize the merits of developing a single market for rail services and the potential benefits for consumers. the granting of a derogation to the geographically separate rail networks of ireland, northern ireland and greece should in no way undermine this principle. on the other hand, the removal of the derogation would impose an unnecessary administrative burden and costs on those member states, without any visible benefit to the parties concerned.

in writing. -(en) it is a fair political objective for the european union to ensure that businesses conduct their business in an environmentally friendly way. the eu's environmental management and audit system, known as emas, has given eu companies the opportunity to be certified as implementing good environmental practice in their activities. today we are discussing the revision of the original 1993 regulation which established the community environmental management and audit system. the criteria for admission to the emas register are very strict. the fact is that the results obtained by the national standardisation authorities of each member state of the european union are made available to the public. each national standardisation authority shall carry out a detailed and comprehensive analysis of all activities of a potential emas candidate that may affect the environment. the new regulation under discussion today will make it easier for small and mediumsized enterprises to apply for emas accreditation. overall, only large industrial companies have so far sought recognition under the community environmental management and audit system. we must remember that emas is the european version of the iso standard known as iso 14001. the iso standard is essentially the equivalent of iso 9000 for european companies, but with the appropriate environmental perspective. the correct policy of the european union is to ensure that all european companies respect the environment and ensure that they have the necessary practices in place to ensure that their activities do not have a negative impact on the environment in any form. however strict the regulations, there needs to be more flexibility in the system to ensure that all sectors and all companies in the european union have a chance of being accredited under the community environmental management and audit scheme. i fully support this programme and recommend this report.
in writing. -the main objective of this recommendation is to enhance compliance with and contribute to a more consistent implementation and application of community environmental legislation in the member states. this draft recommendation sets out the minimum criteria to be applied to the organisation, implementation, monitoring and publication of the results of environmental inspections. it provides for environmental inspections of all industrial and other enterprises whose emissions and discharges into the environment are subject to special authorization or licensing. this is certainly the case for all airlines that receive pollution control licences under the auspices of environmental protection agencies. according to the text of this recommendation, environmental inspections will include: site visits, verification of internal control operations carried out by the operators of the controlled installations, inspection of infrastructure, equipment and proper environmental management on the ground, as well as records kept by the operators. two types of inspections are envisaged: regular inspections as part of a planned inspection programme and spot inspections to follow up complaints related to the granting and renewal or amendment of different licences. member states will be required to draw up plans for environmental inspection activities covering their entire territory and facilities located therein. these plans will be made available to the public through the wider and more general information campaigns envisaged in this proposal. according to this draft recommendation, each plan should define the geographical area it covers, cover a defined time period, include specific provisions for its revision, identify the specific sites or types of installations concerned, establish the schedules of regular environmental inspections, provide for and outline the procedures related to spot environmental inspections and provide for coordination between the different inspection authorities. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, caroline jackson, on her report. it calls for the proposal to be converted into a directive which would be applied uniformly throughout the european union. the rapporteur believes that only a directive can oblige member states to introduce such inspection systems. as a member of the european parliament's committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, i support the need for member states to establish harmonised environmental inspections. the european council must follow the opinion of the european parliament on this matter. we must not forget that in environmental matters the european parliament has the co-decision power which derives from the application of the provisions of the treaty of amsterdam. the european union also has competence in environmental matters in accordance with article 130 of the maastricht treaty.

- mr president, if anyone needs any kind of proof that the permanent sanctions against iraq are something very bad, we just have to look at the number of resignations among very influential people in the united nations. we can also take into account the words of mary robinson, who strongly condemned the situation created by the sanctions. the fact is that victims, innocent people, women and children, people without any power, without any kind of control whatsoever, are persecuted. there is a permanent silent war against the iraqi people which has received little attention, and i am glad to see that some people in this parliament have actually visited iraq to see the situation for themselves. this is extremely important. the illegal bombing by the united states and britain, which is not covered by any un security council resolution, is totally unjustified and absolutely outrageous: it should not be allowed to continue. the fact that we can allow an illegal bombing like this to continue is hardly believable. the iraqi infrastructure has been completely decimated. once again, the victims are innocent people who have no power. i would also like to congratulate some of the organizations in our communities that are doing something or trying to raise awareness about the problem, and i would like to mention here those voices in the desert that are trying to break the sanctions - i think they play a vital role and i would like to assure them of my support. we must also consider that whatever measures are taken against iraq in the future, we must focus on improving human rights and consider protecting the kurds in the north and the shiites in the south with a view to guaranteeing them a certain degree of autonomy. we must also keep them in mind.

- mr president, the pregnant woman and her unborn child, the women who have just given birth and the women who are breastfeeding must be supported as much as possible during this very important period, not only for the mother and her child, but also for society, given the low birth rate and the ageing of the population in europe today. it is clear that employers have social responsibilities, but governments should lead by example, especially in countries like mine, where the role of the mother and the role of the family are protected by the constitution. unfortunately, the situation seems to be different in ireland with regard to paid maternity leave, measures to facilitate breastfeeding and the exemption from night work. i quote these examples to emphasize that it is not enough to adopt a directive for it to be applied. it is high time we stopped making empty promises to working mothers, their children and their families in our european community.
- mr president, i think most people would agree that the existing laws are far too lenient and it is important that some sort of provision is put in place to ensure that criminal trials are possible. at present, there is absolutely no incentive for companies to redress their actions and take responsibility to ensure that negligence does not occur. this situation causes a chain reaction which affects other companies. when it comes to litigation, citizens themselves have to take on the legal responsibility, which can be extremely difficult and costly. of course, individuals can oppose companies that have enormous powers and resources, which in many cases makes it difficult for them to pursue the lawsuits to their end and ensure that the companies pay the price. these are truly criminal enterprises that, through negligence or deliberate action, destroy the environment, affect human health, wildlife, and plants and cause long-term damage in many different ways, all in the name of profit. in many cases, for example, the transport companies on land or at sea have no sense of responsibility. it is therefore necessary to provide for certain sanctions to ensure that they are compelled to act responsibly. governments should provide more information and make the public aware of the extent and impact of these environmental crimes, because many sections of our society are unaware of them. with regard to water, soil, health, flora, fauna and, of course, the climate, it is extremely important that the appropriate information is available. moreover, the problem is not limited to companies. sometimes it is the national authorities who ignore the law and behave in a totally negligent manner. i do not have to go beyond the borders of my own country to see that in many cases local authorities and other levels of government are themselves responsible for environmental damage. and when it comes to enforcing the law against companies operating in their region, they are also lax in this area. it seems that profit is put before the interests of the people and the environment. this situation should not last and the authorities who fail to enforce the law should themselves be held responsible for the resulting environmental damage. whether it is by negligence or by deliberate action, in the end it does not change much, for the wrong is done. in many cases, companies use the excuse of negligence rather than admitting that an act was deliberate, but in fact there is something deliberate about negligence. these companies knew the consequences of their actions and should therefore be held liable for all costs necessary to remedy, where possible, the damage they caused. i would also like to point out that there are companies, such as british nuclear fuels, which have absolutely no sense of public responsibility and no environmental awareness either. for a very long time they have been the source of pollution along our coasts, polluting the irish sea and endangering the people of ireland and the west coast of the united kingdom. it is enough to recall what happened in chernobyl, a disaster that was again due to a careless attitude. how long before we can correct the shot? such situations can no longer be tolerated in a world where we should be fully aware of the environmental problems for which we are responsible. we have the technology, the know-how and the resources to prevent such things from happening. erika is another example of negligence. i would also like to support the comments made by the last speaker on the transport of animals. this deliberate exploitation and mistreatment of animals should also be considered. at the international level, we have seen many environmental disasters in the past. the bhopal disaster is one example. cutting costs and rounding corners has created the biggest ecological disaster in history. this company has not yet been properly and legally held responsible. she was sued in india. the company manager was supposed to be arrested and a warrant had been issued for his arrest. he fled to the united states where he decided to settle and did not return to india to face the consequences of these acts, despite an interpol arrest warrant. there is also the problem of forests and fisheries. negligent practices in many fishing activities should be taken into account, as this is also an ecological crime with long-term consequences. it is high time that we all took responsibility, because we must not only think of ourselves and our environment, but also of future generations. the only way to achieve this is to ensure that environmental degradation is considered one of the most serious crimes of our society.
- mr president, most of the time when my irish colleague, mrs mckenna, speaks in this house, we disagree. however, on such matters, we have more things that unite us than things that divide us. i certainly acknowledge that the most serious environmental disasters are the result of irresponsibility or the actions of those who have profit as their sole motivation, without regard for the consequences. sadly, such disasters affect many countries at the same time. we know all too well that ecological disasters do not respect international boundaries. in recent times we have seen the problems caused by the pollution of the danube by cyanide from romanian mines, the rupture of the erika reservoir along the atlantic coast of france and, in ireland, the persistent problems caused by sellafield. and of course, in the recent past, there was chernobyl, the memory of which continues to haunt us. events like this will continue to happen until we have established the full 'polluter pays' principle. the multinationals will only change their behaviour if they are forced to pay the price for their misdeeds. they will continue to inflict damage on the environment as long as the reward far outweighs the punishment. their attitude is based on the simple belief that ensuring restrictive environmental protection is detrimental to their economic activities or significantly increases their overheads. furthermore, the small fines they are forced to pay are nothing compared to the huge profits they make. that is why i welcome this initiative which recognises the international nature of the damage. it also recognises the need for severe sanctions. the penalisation of environmental damage will send a clear warning to those responsible. if the danish proposals are to be implemented, those who allow dangerous vessels to go to sea will think twice. others, such as the sellafield officials, who deliberately conceal information and engage in questionable practices will be held accountable for their actions. and the directors and managers of multinational companies who make important decisions that show their blatant disregard for the consequences of those decisions would risk having their businesses shut down due to heavy fines and the seizure of their assets, as well as being sentenced to a prison term. i think that is the direction we must go in, but i would also like to add a point. proposals and actions in this field should not be confined to the territory of the european union. we have a responsibility to ensure that multinationals will be unable to export their damage and their behaviour to developing countries because of the restrictions we can impose on them within the european union. we must also impose standards on european companies operating in developing countries and seek international cooperation so that standards and harsh sanctions can be applied globally.

i would like to speak on behalf of the non-federalists in our group who actually feel that europe is centralising far too much and more and more powers are being taken away from people at local and national levels, basically without any proper democratic control. there is quite a lot of double-talk here tonight. on the one hand, people are saying that we are not talking about or aiming for a single diplomatic corps, but it is quite clear that the aim of this whole project is to have eu embassies abroad and to abolish national ones. i am not being nationalistic and saying that i want the irish embassy, but there is a certain amount of danger in this, particularly because it is geared towards the common foreign and security policy, which is not in the interest of the developing world or of the peoples that have been persecuted and exploited by member states of this union. you only need to look at the number of, for example, british embassies abroad. britain has twice as many embassies as either france or germany. it has a huge diplomatic corps which reflects its colonial past. i have grave reservations about the idea of a common european embassy, given that in the past people fleeing persecution in certain countries were able to go into some embassies and seek asylum and get protection. what happens if you have a single european diplomatic presence abroad? we abolish national embassies. what would happen then if someone fleeing from persecution were to seek asylum or help from the eu embassy if it did not suit the eu's interests to support him or her? i believe that there are grave dangers in what we are doing. it is interesting to hear some people speaking today about the need for a higher profile and eu embassies to compete with the us. this is all about competition. it is about being able to compete with other superpowers. i do not approve of the idea that europe should create another superpower. to do so would destabilise the global community. whereas, we should look for more cooperation and less competition. the idea of competing with the us is completely unacceptable. if you look at the report itself, it is geared towards facilitating the common foreign and security policy. not all greens and efa members in our group – the non-federalists – would support mr dupuis' amendments, because we believe that they are moving in the same direction and we would have grave reservations about that.
mr president, first of all i should like to congratulate mr imbeni on an excellent report, into which he put a tremendous amount of work. i want to refer to the question of third world debt relief. we must be more forceful in our efforts to put the issue of debt relief for third world countries on a permanent footing. the jubilee 2000 campaign helped to focus international attention on the debt issue. the campaign has been instrumental in achieving progress in providing additional relief both at multilateral level and by major bilateral creditors. poor countries face unsustainable debt burdens at the same time as they are confronting natural disasters, famine and the hiv/aids pandemic. debt service payments often exceed health and education budgets. surely there is nothing more important in developing countries than education. if people have literacy and numeracy they are a danger to the leadership of the worst-governed countries. the world bank and the international poverty reduction needs to be substantially strengthened. debt relief programmes for third world countries must be put in place permanently. the joint united nations programmes on aids has already madam president, thank you very much indeed for arranging this very important debate at this particular time. i also wish to warmly welcome to our parliament the speaker of the knesset, mr avraham burg, and the president of the palestinian legislative council, mr ahmed qurie. like many people, i too, madam president, was disappointed that the recent middle east peace talks in camp david did not culminate in a peace settlement between the palestinians and the israeli government. however, i do not believe that these peace talks were a complete failure either. we should fully recognise that the palestinians and the israeli government are now negotiating on bottom line issues, including the status of jerusalem, the borders of the palestinian state, the fate of jewish settlements and the right of return of palestinian refugees. i very much hope that the middle east will resume peace talks in the near future. i appeal to the key parties involved to strive in good faith to lay down a negotiated settlement based on existing agreements. permanent status negotiations resumed in september 1999. it was always going to take time to tease through what were very difficult, delicate and sensitive political matters. eu heads of state have affirmed on a number of occasions that the security of the israeli and palestinian people both collectively and individually must always be protected. i support the principle of exchanging land for peace and the continuing and unqualified palestinian right to self-determination. i believe that the best way to address this latter issue is through negotiation. i am calling on the israeli government and the palestinian leadership to take the necessary steps to re-establish mutual confidence, accept the need to make concessions and focus on the overriding goal of achieving lasting peace and stability in the region. the peace talks between israel and the palestinians are not the only matter up for discussion within the middle east. it is important that negotiations between israel and syria should resume quickly and that the lebanese track of the middle east peace process should also be addressed. in this latter regard, i welcome the fact that since the israeli forces left southern lebanon, the region has been very peaceful. there has been peaceful progress on the lebanese-israeli border since the lebanese government gained authority within the region, and we all must certainly welcome the fact that southern lebanon is now at peace. in the overall context, securing peace in the middle east is always going to take time. the first step in the current peace process was taken when israel and egypt concluded the camp david peace treaty in 1979. it took another twelve years before a comprehensive peace process got under way with the madrid conference, which enshrined the land for peace principle. we can all take solace from the fact that the key protagonists in this difficult political equation are now addressing the most vexed and thorny bottom line issues that have to be resolved if a permanent peace agreement is to be put in place in the middle east.
i fully endorse the statements by the president-in-office and commissioner patten. i cannot understand how they have ignored in their contribution the conditions in which the people of iraq now find themselves. the reasons for this are twofold: the continued dictatorship of saddam hussein and his oppression of iraq's citizens and the continued imposition of sanctions of the most draconian kind. the war has always been about controlling oil and in my view little or nothing to do with kuwait. as for weapons of mass destruction, i agree that somebody like saddam hussein should not have control of them, but there are others in the region who should not either. look around the middle east, and indeed the rest of the world, and what confronts us is an array of weapons of mass destruction, mostly supplied by the developed world, including the european union. the market in weapons is now so competitive that some of the most horrific despots are getting bargain basement deals for weapons of mass destruction. it is a lesson to the middle east that it is unwise to nationalise the oil industry. it must be asked where did saddam hussein get the weapons from before the gulf war and where is he supposed to be getting them from now? i suggest that we should consider appointing a political envoy from the european union to iraq to see if we can broker some sort of peace, between the united nations and iraq, because the sanctions are genocidal in their effect.
mr moscovici, commissioner patten, i also would like to be associated with the words of welcome to our honoured guests, mr ahmed qurie and mr avraham burg. it is understandable that palestinians feel that right would have been on their side in formerly declaring an independent palestinian state on 13 september. they believe that the oslo negotiations and the various camp david discussions would have conferred legitimacy on such a step. however, it has to be recognised that even if negotiations had progressed so far that the declaration of statehood was non-controversial, the future of jerusalem was always going to re-ignite controversy once again. jerusalem is the key issue and it needs to be looked at in a new light. it is a unique problem which requires a unique response. jerusalem should not be regarded as an arab city or a jewish city, a christian city or a muslim city. progress will only be made if it is agreed that it is a truly international city to which all traditions have a valid claim. the governance of this historic city must be removed from any nationalistic or sectarian, in the best sense of that word, spirit and this governance must provide for equal access and rule for all. just as we were delighted to remove berlin walls in europe, we do not want to see similar walls erected in jerusalem. people from the furthest ends of the earth have an affinity with jerusalem and this must be recognised and honoured and not overlooked in the current negotiations. with these few words, i would like to wish the discussions, if they do take place, all success and genuinely hope that they lay the basis for permanent peace in the middle east.
