an important european value that we all, whether lay or religious, purport to share is that any experimentation on a human being should only be for their exclusive and direct benefit. we depart from that principle at our peril and here we clearly have departed from it. we cannot experiment on human beings at any stage of their development and we certainly cannot mass-produce embryos for experimentation. the next stage will be commercial exploitation, which our own bio-patenting directive allows for. the uk authorities argue that in spite of ethical doubts the cloning of human embryos is necessary because it is the only way to help patients suffering from various diseases. many scientists dispute this and recommend more research with adult stem cells to achieve the same results in curing diseases. can we not get together and find a way of dealing with stem research without experimenting directly on human beings? i hope this house agrees that this is a value that europe was built on.
as you know, i am a great admirer of yours and i am sure that you were as uncomfortable repeating your answer today as i was hearing it. i know of your own personal commitment but actually you have not given me any clear indication of what the commission is actually going to do in the un conference which is taking place in a couple of weeks time. having listened to the extent of plans that the commission has in relation to animal welfare, i can only wish that they had similar plans in relation to the welfare of children. accepting the legal limits within which you have to work, could you in fact give me any indication that anybody from the commission for example is going to be present at this united nations conference in september? in what context and are they proposing to make any input into the conference?
the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. the question put by the honourable member is whether coillte is entitled to receive cofinanced aid from the guarantee section of the agricultural fund to compensate for income losses in connection with afforestation measures under regulation 2080 of 1992. i would like to make it clear that there is no judgment of the court on this particular question. however, it is true that the commission classifies this company coillte as a state-owned company because this company is owned by the irish state. this does not entitle the person to receive aid to compensate for loss of income. the aid is reserved for farmers and other natural or legal persons governed by private law, in accordance with article 2 (2) (b) of the afforestation regulation. the commission therefore intends to refuse to co-finance the aid paid with retroactive effect from 1 august 1996. the total amount for 1997 and 1998 is about eur 4.8 million. this financial correction was contested by the irish authorities in the so-called conciliation procedure. the conciliation body is still working on this issue and has not yet concluded its examination of the matter. in this procedure, the commission takes the view that the irish authorities have not yet provided any evidence that coillte is to be classified as a private company. the commission cannot confirm the amount of £6.5 million mentioned by the honourable member, nor the amount of £30.5 million which appears to be for future payments. the commission will, however, continue to take the same position as it has taken up to now in all future applications.
i am very glad to hear the commission's reply because the funding that coillte has was set up by the state, it was the state that set coillte up. it is a semi-state company and it is responsible for forestry development. now it actually used the money it was getting from these interest payments on land that they were purchasing. what is interesting as well as that the chairman, ray macsharry is a former european commissioner for agriculture and he is still refusing to acknowledge the fact that coillte is a public entity and that it is not entitled to such payments. at a recent meeting with coillte i was told that the government is gong to challenge this decision. i believe it is actually a waste of taxpayers' money to challenge it because i think it is quite clear to anybody with an ounce of common sense that they are not entitled to this money. what is really devastating is that they have actually prevented the people who should have got the money from rural depopulation for ensuring that people who did lose income actually got the money and i would like to see the commission ensure that coillte get no more money because they were not entitled to it in the first place and that they have to pay it back. they are now saying that the state will have to pay it back which is not acceptable either.
mr president, this is a framework directive with ambitious targets for the protection and improvement of water quality, taking account of the polluter-pays principle. we will not have the entire picture until the relevant daughter directives are in place over the next few years. water pricing policies by the year 2010 will have to provide adequate incentives for users to use water resources efficiently. the new paragraph 4 of article 9 will allow for the continuation of the established practice in ireland of not applying water charges to the domestic water sector. you can only govern with the consent of the people and by 2010 domestic water charges could well be acceptable to the irish electorate. at the moment, it is a hugely politically-charged issue. i thank my colleagues for their understanding, particularly mrs schleicher, who represented our case with understanding through all stages, especially at conciliation. the principle of subsidiarity in relation to water pricing policy is jealously guarded in ireland, but public opinion will probably change by 2010. if we do not apply household or domestic water charges by then we will have to explain our action to the commission. there is growing appreciation in ireland that clean drinking water is a scarce resource and that all services, particularly the cost of distributing clean water to all households, have to be paid for. at the moment the central exchequer or the taxpayer, is paying for this, yet many do not benefit. many have to pay to sink their own pump in ireland and pay the electricity charges. many are on group water schemes which are not part of the public water supply and have to pay dearly each year for the privilege of rather questionable quality water being distributed to their houses. so we are talking about a question of equity and changing public opinion and we appreciate the matter being left to the irish government to decide and to influence public opinion.
mr president, the over-commercialisation of sport has clearly led to the exploitation of many young athletes. proven cases of doping in sporting competitions have diminished the reputation of many sports in the eyes of the public. at last, the international olympic committee has faced up to its obligations to combat the widespread use of drugs in sport. up to 2500 drug tests will be carried out during the sydney games. i welcome the ioc's announcement that heavy penalties will be imposed on sportsmen and women caught using drugs in sydney this month and in the future. the way to stop the use of drugs in sport should be coordinated on a worldwide basis. the union must continue to work through the world anti-doping agency to ensure that uniform and structured guidelines are implemented for all sporting activities. in conclusion, we should recall that the treaty of amsterdam emphasises the social significance of sport, but the european union has no powers under the treaty to take action on sport. that is an issue that should be addressed in the ongoing discussions on the reform of union policies and initiatives.
in writing. - i support this report, but i would like to make some reservations. the derogation granted to ireland in respect of water pricing is not as strong as i would like. the pivotal article of the directive, article 9 (4), states that a member state is not in breach of the directive if the decision not to introduce water pricing based on established national practices does not prejudice the aims and achievement of the objectives of the directive. it follows from this paragraph that a decision by ireland not to impose a water charge would constitute an infringement of the directive if that decision can be linked to the failure of the irish government to meet the new standards. given the irish government's failure to date, i have serious doubts about its ability to meet the new standards.
in writing. - i support the conciliation agreement between parliament and the european council on the water framework directive. it consolidates existing national and community legislation on surface water and groundwater safety. the key elements of this directive ensure the protection of surface water, coastal water, waste water and groundwater in the short and long term. initially, the european council, which represents the 15 eu governments, did not include any provisions on the disposal of hazardous substances in this new water directive. i support the result of this conciliation because it guarantees the inclusion of the issue of the disposal of dangerous substances in the directive, thus eliminating the imbalance. from the irish point of view, we are working on the assumption that the disposal of hazardous substances also includes the prohibition of the discharge of radioactive substances. water pricing was already a sensitive point in the directive. today it obliges member states to take into account the principle of recovery of costs of water-related services, including environmental and resource costs, in accordance with the polluter-pays principle. member states should ensure by 2010 that water pricing policies provide sufficient incentives for users to use water resources efficiently. member states will be allowed, exceptionally, to waive the obligation to recover costs where this waiver is in accordance with 'established national practice'. water pricing policy is also a fiscal issue. in accordance with the existing eu treaties, such a change in the tax regime requires the unanimous support of eu governments. it is quite normal and justified that this tax issue remains within the competence of the member states; the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the taxation of capital goods, such as the introduction of a new tax on capital goods. the substantial improvement in water quality will be one of the consequences of the implementation of the directive.
in writing. - i welcome the agreement reached on this directive between parliament and the european council, which represents the 15 governments of the european union. we know that up to nine million vehicles are scrapped in the eu every year. this directive, which will be uniformly enacted throughout the european union, will ensure that cars are disposed of in a more environmentally friendly way in the future. it has been decided that by 2015 all vehicles in the european union will have to be fully recyclable. this is justified, since the european union has an obligation, both at national and international level, to apply more environmentally friendly legislation. up to 75% of all metal components in new vehicles are recyclable as scrap metal. this must be pursued in order to encourage higher environmental standards. the aim of the directive is to increase the recycling of vehicles and to encourage the reuse of car components. this requires improving the construction of various commercial motor vehicles and installing dismantling infrastructure in the european union. the common position allows the last owner of a vehicle to hand over his vehicle to a treatment facility free of charge. manufacturers are obliged to cover all or a large part of the costs of implementing these measures and to take back end-of-life vehicles at their own expense. during the conciliation process, the european parliament and the 15 eu governments agreed that this provision would apply from 1 january 2007. however, member states are entirely free to implement it earlier. car manufacturers, suppliers and equipment manufacturers must make an effort to limit the use of hazardous substances. the use of recycled materials in car manufacturing must be considered from the design stage. during the conciliation, the european parliament and the european council also voted in favour of a ban on the use of heavy materials such as cadmium or lead in vehicles manufactured after 1 july 2003. motor vehicle manufacturers should also provide information on the rate of reuse, recycling and reconditioning of end-of-life vehicles that will be achieved in the coming years. in conclusion, i would say that i have received many requests in ireland about the need to take account of the vintage car sector in europe. at the request of the european parliament, vintage vehicles will be explicitly excluded from the scope of this directive. this is very important in light of the key socio-economic role of the vintage car sector in promoting european tourism.
mr president, earlier this year, i had the honour to be part of a delegation from the european parliament to new delhi and nepal. arising from our visit and in particular our visit to the refugee camps in eastern nepal, the delegation gave a commitment that they would do everything possible to raise the issue of the continuing plight of the bhutanese refugees in nepal. i would like to assure the members of the house that the refugees are fully aware of, and greatly value and appreciate, the earlier efforts of the european parliament in drawing attention to their situation. this was clearly demonstrated to us during our visit to the region, and our resolve to continue to encourage an agreement now between bhutan and nepal is as strong as ever. the influx of bhutanese refugees into nepal began in late 1991 and the fear of diminishing international attention, therefore, is always present. the refugees have been waiting for the last ten years for an amicable and permanent solution to the crisis. as ms mccarthy has said – very many of them want to go home. there have been numerous bilateral talks but so far a final political settlement has not been secured. in new york, the united nations millennium summit is taking place as we speak and it offers the prime ministers of bhutan and nepal an opportunity to come together and to demonstrate their commitment to the ideas of peace and tolerance by agreeing to an early solution to the problem of bhutanese refugees in nepal. this would be a fitting way to mark the un's millennium summit. i hope they will be given every encouragement to do so. one of the crucial issues to be decided is the definition of the family unit for verification purposes. nepal has accepted a compromise put forward by the unhcr who has been playing an important role in seeking to help to resolve the impasse. i strongly urge the bhutanese authorities to accept the compromise so that field verification can begin immediately. while the european union remains one of the major donors, both the unhcr and the world food programme are finding it increasingly difficult to raise funds to run the refugee camps. i would urge international donors to continue to make sufficient funds available to permit the running of the camps during the negotiation and the verification process. at the same time, i would expect the donors to insist that the bhutanese government facilitate a rapid repatriation of the refugees. i very much welcome the news conveyed to the house by ms mccarthy of the generosity of the japanese people in this particular respect. we were assured that bilateral negotiations would be speedily concluded and that actual verification in the camps would begin last july. that agreement regrettably is still outstanding. i therefore reiterate once more our appeal to all those concerned to take the necessary political initiatives to achieve a lasting solution to the unacceptable plight of the bhutanese refugees, of whom over 17 000 have been born in the camps.

i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs wallis, for her persistence. it has been a long and hard road. the european commission has made a proposal which would ensure that consumers would have the right to take action in their own national courts and legal disputes arising from cross-border e-commerce transactions. amendments from the legal affairs committee before parliament would take that right and make it subject to unreasonable conditions and i cannot support those amendments. the amendments shift the balance in the report towards traders and away from consumers. consumers can agree – prior to the conclusion of the contract – that they cannot bring proceedings against the other party in the courts of the member states where they are domiciled. the main problem with this approach is that it assumes that consumers first read the small print in such contracts and, even if they do, that they fully understand the implications of agreeing to forego the right to sue in their own courts and the differences in relation to costs incurred, legal systems, the choice of language used and so on. it is unlikely that the average consumer understands or is aware and we cannot agree to their full legal rights being abrogated in this way. i agree with mrs wallis that commissioner byrne was right to say that he would not accept this, and i hope that commissioner vitorino feels the same way, because this would be a disastrous outcome. the committee did, however, agree to take a coherent approach to the law relating to the internet. under the proposal, key information contained in the court files in the member states would be copied, using a standard form, to a centralised database for on-line access by interested parties. such a system will also promote the convergence and coordination of the different national legal systems and enable parties to check on the status of trading partners and suppliers, especially in an age of on-line trading. this european causebook is very much to be welcomed and i hope that we will be able to support this report.
i want to compliment mrs wallis on the preparation and the presentation of her report. i broadly welcome this report and i want to focus in particular on amendment no 20, recital 26 (new), which states that the commission will consider proposals for the establishment of a centralised electronic european union causebook and judgment registry database. this has been justified very clearly in the report. it is pro-citizen and it is pro-consumer and i would strongly urge the commissioner to consider this proposal favourably, particularly in view of the fact that it is so pro-consumer and pro-citizen. i would strongly urge the commission to consider it favourably as we now live in a global network with a global economy, global companies. but we have yet to establish an infrastructure to enable the brussels convention, as amended and extended, to benefit our eu consumer citizens and to attract inward investment. it is necessary to establish the causebook which has been inspired by a proposal from an irish lawyer, twinkle egan who has worked diligently on this initiative for some years. at the expense of repeating myself, this will ensure that it is to the citizens’ advantage. this system has the advantage of having already been vetted by many reputable institutions including the international bar association. the system has the attraction of being capable, of extension, to synchronising the international legal system of the global world in which we now live and trade. the system would enable citizens of the community and others to have prompt and easy access to authoritative and up-to-date information in relation to the existence and progress of trans-border litigation in which they may have a legitimate interest. this system would also provide efficiency in obtaining and enforcing judgments within the eu.
i would like to join my colleagues in thanking mrs wallis for her tremendous effort and work on this very difficult brief and i also want to support the call by my colleague, pat the cope gallagher, and my other irish colleague, nuala ahern, with regard to the establishment of a centralised european union electronic database on case-law, put forward by twinkle egan, a barrister from ireland. however, when we look at the difficulties which have arisen with regard to this report, when we look at the disparate views – the so-called purists on behalf of the consumers or the other so-called purists on behalf of business and e-commerce – we sometimes forget that a lot of small- and medium-sized enterprises are consumers themselves, who will be trading with larger companies or larger suppliers and who deserve the same protection that i, “as an individual” or anybody else, as an individual consumer, would have. therefore, what is most of all required within the new framework is certainty with regard to the protection available and the responsibilities of the people who are trading on-line. i look to other legislation to see what analogies or what benefits can be drawn from it. under the various insurance directives, for instance, non-national insurance companies are required to have an agent in each member state to deal with insurance claims that may occur in that member state. why not put in place a similar requirement on on-line trading companies? likewise, under the consumer credit directive, the provider of the service is required to inform the consumer of certain rights, certain entitlements, certain get-out clauses as well. it is not difficult to transpose that kind of ideology into a direct consumer-service-provider relationship also. that is why, more than anything else today, we need to put this regulation in place. we do not need to defer it any longer. we do not need to put any more mud into the water. we need to enable europe, which is lagging behind in e-commerce and internet business worldwide, to put in place new arrangements with regard to creation of on-line business and the protection of consumer rights and also to ensure that europe takes its rightful place at the top of the world trading system of internet and on-line businesses. that is why it is important that we should support the general opinion, agreed by the legal affairs committee and as proposed by mrs wallis, concerning those areas over which there has been some conflict, i take note of what commissioner byrne said recently in his speech about the other opportunities. no consumer, “as an individual” or as a company, can be denied the ultimate right to take legal action but that does not mean that this should be the first course of action. there should be an agreed dispute-resolution mechanism available at local level, which can reduce the cost of settling consumer disputes because, as has been pointed out by many speakers this evening, there is the question of added costs with regard to resolution of disputes. finally, i would once again like to thank the rapporteur and the commission representatives for their work and remind all members that there is another way under the brussels i convention and we can put other mechanisms in place. let us agree on this report and then make changes in other areas as they come before us.

madam president, the decision of the danish people on 28 september on participation in the european currency regime will undoubtedly figure at the biarritz european council meeting. eu member state governments must now seriously reflect on what has happened in denmark. the danish decision comes two months before eu leaders are to meet in nice to reform existing eu treaties and policies. i support the enlargement of the european union. it will help consolidate democratic and civil structures in eastern and central europe, expand market economies and help build a european continent at peace with itself. however, eu member state governments face difficult choices. we cannot deny that the danish people used the recent referendum as a test of confidence in the structures of the european union. the referendum in denmark had more to do with the direction of the european union and future political integration than actually participating in the euro currency itself. serious concern was expressed among danish voters that the elimination of tax vetoes within the european union would dissipate future social security benefits. i do not necessarily support this. however, the point remains the same: namely that the european member state governments, the european commission and the european parliament have a serious battle on their hands to persuade the 370 million citizens of the european union that greater political and economic integration is the way forward for the european union. this is the core issue. if member state governments push the issue of political integration too far in nice, we will have real difficulties in securing approval in the referendums which will have to be held in various european union countries. any reform of european union treaties must be fair and balanced. it must protect the interests of the smaller member states, and we must not help build a two-tier europe where larger member states run the european union at the expense of the smaller member states. we must also protect national cultural and linguistic diversity.
mr president, on behalf of my group i want to state that the implementation of a new eu charter of fundamental rights will certainly raise the status and visibility of human rights in the european union. it is stated in article 6 of the treaty of amsterdam that fundamental rights exist within the union and must be respected. citizens from the candidate countries in central and eastern europe can also draw inspiration from such a charter. however, we should recall that the european convention on human rights, which all member states have signed and adopted in their national law, has now been operating for nearly 50 years and works hand in hand with the un declaration on human rights. the european court of human rights in strasbourg adjudicates on possible human right infringements in signatory countries. the european convention on human rights has been a proven success. the citizens from member states of the countries of the echr have confidence in this convention and jurisprudence has built up around it. our primary concern with regard to the charter of fundamental rights is that it must not conflict with the european convention. this in itself would lead to greater legal uncertainty, which would be highly unwelcome. the division lines must be drawn between the european court of human rights in strasbourg and the court of justice in luxembourg. the most difficult problem facing this new charter is not its actual content but whether it has legal status. a binding and all-embracing charter with completely legally enforceable rights could result in difficulties at a later stage. certainly in ireland constitutional amendments would be necessary if elements of the draft charter were to be legally enforceable. referenda might also be needed in other member states. this could lead to further uncertainty and failure to ratify at member state level.
counsel. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, let me begin by thanking you for organising this debate on enlargement, which i welcome for two reasons. firstly, because - and i do not need to remind you of this here - the enlargement of the european union, as mr brok has just said, is not a priority like any other. this is the background, the perspective of all the activities of the european union today, a political perspective which conditions all our work, starting naturally with the work on the reform of the institutions. and i do not want to separate the debate we had this morning from the debate this afternoon. in this connection, i would like to welcome the work done by the european parliament in the form of a report on each of the 12 candidate countries that have entered into negotiations and an overall report by president brok, which i shall have the opportunity to refer to in more detail in my speech. secondly, it seems to me that, on the basis of the work undertaken by previous presidencies and by the commission, the french presidency will be able, i hope at least, to give a fairly precise view of the accession process at the european council in nice. i therefore propose, in order to make a useful contribution to this debate, to tell you very concretely how the french presidency is acting during this six-month period in order to achieve the results i have just indicated. first orientation is here. we must, of course, go as far as possible in the accession negotiations in order to take as accurate an account as possible in nice, country by country and chapter by chapter. in this context, i would first like to remind you that we are planning to hold two negotiating sessions at the level of alternates and one at ministerial level for each candidate country, in two waves, on 21 november and 5 december, in addition to the sessions of the general affairs council. the french presidency will inform the committee you chair, mr brok, of the outcome of these negotiations as soon as tuesday afternoon, as is now the custom among us. as you know, the helsinki european council emphasized the principle of differentiation between the candidate countries, and both the presidency and, i believe, the commission are really working hand in hand on this matter as regards the conduct of the negotiations, and i would like to stress before you the perfect convergence of views that exists with commissioner verheugen, whose very high quality of work i commend here. this means that each application must be judged and will be judged in the end on its own merits. it's the formula. i shall not, of course, go into the details of each of these negotiations, perhaps leaving this presentation to the debate on the 12 reports which will follow. but i would like to recall in a few words the extent of the work that has been undertaken with those who, for the sake of convenience of language - i believe, as you do, that this should be stopped, but there are also chronologies that are still called the luxembourg six and the helsinki six - i will call them the luxembourg six, i say it in quotation marks. with these six, all the chapters of the acquis except one, the chapter on the institutions, have been opened, some of them being provisionally closed, from 12 to 16 depending on the country, others still under negotiation, from 13 to 18 depending on the country. i think we are beginning to get a fairly clear picture of the difficulties that remain in each of these negotiations, both from the point of view of the applicant countries and from the point of view of the union itself, and which can be classified, i think, into three categories. first, there is the restoration of the acquis. what is the situation in the candidate countries? how can we assess in practical terms the commitments they make in the negotiations and their capacity to implement this acquis in practice? the french presidency, like the european parliament, therefore wanted to have regularly updated tables of progress in the adoption of the acquis, on the understanding that the basic principle of the negotiations must remain full adoption of the acquis. the second is the question of transitional periods, which is obviously linked to the previous one. as you know, the candidates have made a number of requests to that effect. this is a sign that the accession negotiations have clearly entered a new phase and that it is now necessary, as the french presidency wishes, to enter into substantive discussions. i would point out in this connection that the council has started, on the basis of a first document from the commission, a process of reflection which should enable the union to make progress in the negotiations. finally, of course, there are the negotiating positions, which are the most difficult chapters of the acquis, particularly in view of their budgetary implications for the union. i am naturally thinking of the common agricultural policy and regional development policies. i have said it and i have no difficulty in repeating it here, the french presidency will not avoid any of the difficulties that arise, which does not mean, of course, that we will have the time and the means to deal with each of them. but for us to give these negotiations a new impetus means that, particularly as regards the question of transitional periods, we will do everything to make progress in a pragmatic way. a few words, then, on the 'helsinki six', still with the quotation marks, which launched the negotiations earlier this year. half of the chapters of the acquis will have been opened for negotiation by the end of our presidency. we are in agreement with commissioner verheugen that we intend to open negotiations on 42 new chapters, i.e. from 4 to 9 for each country, just as the portuguese presidency did before us. this should enable the best candidates to catch up fairly quickly, and perhaps as early as next year, with those of the luxembourg group, and we know that this is a wish that some have explicitly expressed in your benches. it is clear that the work done by each of the rapporteurs and by the parliamentary committees will be useful in feeding our own reflections. let me now turn to the second direction in which the presidency intends to develop the enlargement process during this six-month period. i have just mentioned the very important technical work that has been undertaken over the last three years and i have also said that, as far as at least some of the candidate countries are concerned, we will soon have a fairly precise picture of the difficulties that remain. so we feel that it is time to take this work to a more political level by gathering all the information we have. we are therefore preparing a substantive ministerial debate to take place at the general affairs council on 20 november on the basis of specific documents to be provided by the commission concerning, in particular, the accession of the candidate countries to the acquis and the progress made by each of them. on the basis of this debate, the presidency will have the necessary elements for the heads of state and government to have a serious discussion in nice on the continuation of the enlargement process. it is also in this spirit that the french presidency has decided to organise two meetings of the european conference, which brings together the 15 member states and the 12 + 1 candidate countries - 12 in negotiation and one candidate. one of these meetings will take place at ministerial level on 23 november in sochaux and the other at heads of state or government level on 7 december in nice. these meetings will first of all be an opportunity to inform the candidate countries of the state of play in the reform of the institutions, a reform which interests them primarily because it is the union of which they will be members that is at stake, but also, of course, to engage with these countries in political reflection on the functioning of the enlarged union. of course, it is not excluded that the candidate countries will take advantage of these occasions to raise once again - as you have said, mr president - the question of the date of enlargement. this is, of course, a perfectly legitimate question, but it must be answered correctly and precisely. i would like to remind you that this matter has already been settled in a certain sense. it is settled, since the european council in helsinki set 1 january 2003 as the date on which the union must be ready to welcome the first members, that is to say those who have made the best preparations, provided, of course, that by then a good treaty has been concluded in nice and ratified by the national parliaments. the date of 1 january 2003 was not chosen at random. i do not see why we should go back one way or the other on this one. i am personally convinced that in the debate on the date of the first accessions, the union must avoid - and i say this with the greatest respect to the european parliament - multiplying the effects of the announcement. everyone understands the importance of a date for the candidate countries, because it is both an objective and a motivating factor for them. but 1 january 2003, the only date agreed by the union, is an extremely ambitious target for the candidates and for us, and it is in this perspective that the presidency will be putting all its efforts into action. i think it is far from me, and far from everyone here, that we will have concluded all the negotiations in 2001, which will then allow us to ratify the results in 2002. it is not about that and, as we all know, for some of the applicant countries the negotiations will continue for a few more years. but it is also true that the work that has been undertaken, and which we wish to give a strong impetus to, should make it possible, as from the end of this year, to better understand the general balance of each of the negotiations and, on the other hand, at least for the candidate countries which are most advanced, to clearly identify the difficulties to be solved as a matter of priority in order to bring these negotiations to a successful conclusion. what i propose to you is that we work in this direction with the idea that we will have to consider, in the perspective of 2003, which candidates are ready to join us on that date. finally, a word on turkey, which the union now considers to be clearly part of the enlargement process and the 13th candidate country. i know that mr morillon is currently preparing a report on this country, which we are awaiting with interest. we can only be pleased that a number of obstacles have been overcome in helsinki to the recognition of the country's candidacy. at the same time, we are fully aware of the hypotheses which continue to weigh on this accession, starting with the issues of respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms in this country. progress has been made, but it is still far from meeting the copenhagen criteria. in agreement with the commission on this point too, the french presidency will strive to achieve a twofold result: firstly, the adoption of an accession partnership, which is necessary for both turkey and the union, and secondly, the establishment of the financial regulation which will enable the union to honour the commitments it has entered into with regard to that country, which we hope to adopt by the end of the year. i share your sentiment, mr president: the european union must not have a petty attitude or a shopkeeper's spirit towards enlargement. the enlargement is a historic project. enlargement is a vast prospect which we must approach with great generosity and vision. but at the same time, we must be aware that we must set up the conditions for its success, both in relation to the candidate countries, in relation to our own views and in relation to what we all want, namely the maintenance of sound common policies. enlargement is our future, provided it does not lead to the dilution or weakening of the community acquis and the common policies to which we are all committed. that is why we must move forward decisively by choosing the side of enlargement and setting the conditions for its success. i am, of course, quite prepared to follow the very important debate which brings us together today.
the commission. mr president of the council! i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community. i welcome the opportunity for a detailed debate on the state and prospects of the negotiations on the enlargement of the european union. i thank the rapporteur for the committee on foreign affairs, mr brok, for his comprehensive and clear report. thank you to all the other reporters. we can already see that despite the differences that may well exist in the details, there is a common basic line. the debate is a welcome opportunity to reaffirm the determination to achieve this enlargement. the european development has entered a phase in which we need more than ever clarity of objectives and determination in action. the two great tasks of reforming internally and opening up externally are closely linked. both are indispensable in themselves, but they must be solved in context if they are to be solved correctly. i would therefore like to state at the outset that the most important building block for completing the enlargement project is now agreement on the eu's institutional reforms. without these reforms, our institutions will suffer a heart attack and will no longer serve their purpose. the successful conclusion of the intergovernmental conference in nice in december will pave the way for even more dynamic negotiations. but success is not just about timing, as important as it is. not every outcome of the igc would be able to cope with the enormous adjustment pressure of enlargement. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's external relations are not affected by the crisis. the new arguments would be used by the eurosceptic forces that are also present there, and populists would arise and claim that it is now finally clear that the eu does not really want the new members. such arguments are hardly to be answered with words. we must convince with action! the greatest enlargement in the history of european unification is morally necessary, strategically necessary and politically feasible. the project is already well advanced, it is irreversible. in no case is the question of 'if' in the negotiations, it is only a question of 'how' and 'when'. the enlargement is the only correct answer to two great historical changes in which we are living. it may be a coincidence, but if so, it is a happy coincidence that this debate is taking place on the 10th anniversary of the completion of german unity. german unity must also be seen as part of and a precursor to the larger goal of european unification, and it was the same events, the same developments, that made german unity possible, that made it possible for us to be here today in this chamber, at all, talking about the possible membership of ten central and eastern european states in the european union. the european union must be able to take action to help the countries of central and eastern europe to achieve their goals. i do not know how to explain to the peoples of europe, who have only achieved freedom and self-determination in the last decade, that the benefits of european integration should be reserved exclusively for those who after 1945 found themselves on the 'right' side of the iron curtain. (applause from the centre) we will not allow a new division of europe. we do not want the ideological lines of demarcation, with their materialization in insurmountable frontiers, to be replaced by a new frontier, a frontier which would separate rich europe from poor europe. it's more than that. it is about extending peace and stability throughout europe. but not any stability. the soviet empire appeared stable for decades, both externally and internally, but it had long been rotten and fragile because this stability was not based on democracy, the rule of law, human rights and the protection of minorities. it is not by chance, but by a perfectly iron law, that we have peace and stability in europe only where these values are realised and where europe is already united or is in the process of being united. peace and stability in europe are the basis for a happy future for all the peoples of europe. if they are not implemented everywhere in europe, they are in danger everywhere. the second major change we are witnessing and to which europe must prepare is the rapid acceleration of economic development towards a global market and global competition. this change opens up great opportunities which we can exploit all the more effectively if we pool our efforts and pursue our interests together. the ever-growing common market will make us stronger. the enlargement of the eurozone will strengthen the euro. the larger community will have more weight in tackling global challenges. the enlargement of the european union is not a change for the future, which has yet to be seen whether it can be implemented. the benefits are already there, they are visible, and they are benefits for all. i am firmly convinced that without the perspective of european integration, the system transformation in central and eastern europe would not have proceeded so rapidly and successfully. if we can see that the countries in the enlargement process have already achieved the stability of their democratic systems in a short time, then this is a result of the efforts of these peoples themselves. they themselves have made the courageous decision to shake off functionary rule and state economics and to create open societies, modern democracies and functioning market economies. but they have also done so because the prospect of membership of the european union gives them hope of the solidarity of the european peoples and because it offers them a firm political and economic anchorage. the benefits of political change can already be seen in economic developments. the enlargement countries of central and eastern europe were only weakly integrated into the world economy. they now trade between 50 and 70% of their external trade with the eu. we have a large trade surplus with all these countries. this translates into higher tax revenues, higher social security contributions and, most importantly, more jobs. the growth forecasts for the whole region are extremely favourable. growth is expected to be well above the eu average over the next ten years. these rapidly developing new markets create new supply and demand, creating a win-win situation from the outset. i do not think i need to stress here that this promising economic prospect is only possible because of the predictable and politically stable framework conditions. with regard to enlargement, some centuries-old conflicts have been resolved, border problems have been rectified and minority issues have been de-emphasised. these achievements demand a positive response. i am sure we can all agree that the people who have taken on this effort should not be disappointed. i am therefore pleased to say that the enlargement process has gained in quality and speed in the first year of the new commission. the process has already developed a dynamic of its own that can no longer be stopped. let me remind you once again of the principles which guide us and which are not at our disposal. first, we prepare the new members for full and equal membership. enlargement does not create a europe à la carte or a two-class society in the union. new members must meet all the conditions for membership. there are no political discounts. secondly, each country has its own chance in the enlargement process. there are no geographical or political groups. the terms luxembourg group and helsinki group are purely technical and are used only to distinguish between the countries invited to the negotiations in luxembourg in 1997 and those which did not join until helsinki in 1999. but it remains that each country can only achieve accession by its actual progress. thirdly, we are committed to the principle of differentiated negotiation. the opening and closing of chapters will be determined by the actual state of preparation and the actual progress of the negotiations. fourthly, it follows as a further principle that the states which started later have a fair chance of catching up with those which started earlier. but i want to make it clear that we cannot promote catching up by slowing down negotiations with those who are more advanced. we can help countries catch up, but not by sending their neighbors to the waiting room. the commission will present its progress reports for 2000 and a new strategy paper on 8 november. the work is not yet finished. the reports of previous years have set high standards of quality, which we will not fall short of. these reports are not school grades. i will avoid any ranking of candidates in the future. the reports are intended to help the candidates to continue necessary reforms and to reduce remaining deficits. the eu institutions will use the reports to monitor progress and to develop negotiating strategies. without wishing to prejudge the results, i can already today mention some of the trends in the new reports. we are seeing progress on a broad front in securing the political criteria. i do not see any danger of a slide into authoritarian structures in any country. neighbourly relations are developing well and fast. the basic values and rules of democracy are firmly anchored. we will continue to monitor potential risks and unresolved issues. we therefore have a very careful view of the situation of national minorities. we therefore seek to take concrete measures to overcome social discrimination, especially against the roma population, in a number of countries. the commission has also made considerable progress in the economic criteria. almost all of them can already be considered market economies, although the processes of economic reform are not yet fully completed. but this is not just for candidates. a number of countries have also made good progress on the second economic criterion of competitiveness in the internal market, so that they are rapidly approaching accession. as regards the third set of criteria, the ability to adopt and apply the entire eu acquis, much remains to be done. i do not criticize the fact that the public administration is still very often weak and that the legal system does not yet provide the level of legal certainty that we need in the community. it is clear that the transformation in this area is more time consuming than elsewhere. the creation of institutions and structures is not the end of the matter. we need the right people first. you have to find them, you have to train them, and besides, you have to be able to pay them. we are already giving these issues high priority in the pre-accession strategy. we'll increase that. we must ensure that the common acquis is not just on paper, but is also fully applied. i would like to mention in this context a problem which requires special attention and which is a cause of deep concern to me. i am referring to the widespread corruption in a number of countries. i am, of course, aware of the specific socioeconomic causes of corruption. but we also see it where not pathetic income ratios provide an easy pattern of explanation. i don't think corruption is some sort of folkloric element or part of a cultural heritage. in modern societies and modern economies, corruption is a cancer. the disease grows and grows, making what's healthy sick. weak administrative structures, lack of legal certainty and corruption are also negative location factors. they deter foreign investors and prevent faster economic development. it is clear that the strong economic gap between eu members and candidate countries will only be narrowed if there is a constant flow of foreign direct investment into the candidate countries. the negotiation process as such has been significantly accelerated once again in the past year. i would like to point out a misunderstanding which sometimes crept in when talking about speed. the pace of progress cannot be measured simply by the number of chapters negotiated. the material substance of the chapters is decisive. the luxembourg group countries have already opened all the remaining chapters during the portuguese presidency, with the exception of the institutions and the mixed chapters, totalling 174. the helsinki group countries will have at least 84 chapters open by the end of the french presidency, almost half of them. i expect that all the remaining chapters will be opened for at least four countries in this group next year. we are entering a new phase. we now want to start negotiating in the narrower sense, during the french presidency, that is to say, we want to take decisions on requests for transitional periods or derogations. the commission will present further considerations on 8 november on the principles governing the handling of transitional periods and the organisation of the decision-making process. i would like to draw attention to two areas of concern today. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement on the agreement on the conclusion of the agreement. in the field of the internal market, we must ensure that transitional periods are as short as possible in terms of time and content. where large and long-term investments are necessary to achieve eu standards, it should be borne in mind that current members have also used long transition periods in these cases. in view of the current situation in monetary union, i would like to inform you that monetary union is part of the treaty, and therefore part of the negotiations. all the candidates want to join the monetary union. the special provisions of the maastricht treaty apply to this. membership of the eu does not automatically lead to the euro, but the specific criteria must be met. participation in the exchange rate mechanism is in any case conditional and requires a specific decision. we shall now proceed quickly to a consolidation of the negotiations. this will lead to a focus on the open questions, some of which are very difficult. i am referring to agriculture, the environment, regional policy, home affairs and justice, and the budget. in preparation for this, the commission is working on a new monitoring system which will allow us to assess precisely the state of negotiations and actual implementation for each country at any time. i would like to say a few words about a subject which is particularly close to my heart, the dissemination of the project 'enlargement in the societies of the member states and the candidate countries'. it is hard to doubt that we have a mediation problem, even if surveys do not give a very accurate picture. to improve communication, the commission has proposed a communication initiative. the legal and financial conditions for this are in place. we're working on the content now. the initiative will be essentially decentralised, i.e. implemented in the member states and in the candidate countries according to the specific needs and circumstances. we therefore seek the closest possible coordination with the programmes of national governments, parliament and other institutions. the tight financial framework forces us to develop concepts that are strongly oriented towards opinion formers and multipliers. purchased mass communication will be impossible. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this means at the same time a democratization of the whole process. any communication strategy is doomed to failure if it does not address what really moves people. we must therefore identify what people associate with enlargement, what hopes and expectations they have, but also what fears and dangers they face. no propaganda is going to make a difference here! we need political answers to political questions. they must be taught well! what kind of questions are these? will there be migration and what impact will this have on labour markets? how will the competitive situation in border regions between the former eu and new members change? will there be environmental and social dumping? what will open borders mean in terms of fighting crime and, finally, how will we pay for all this? we have good answers to all these questions. migration movements will decrease overall thanks to enlargement. problems that may arise in the first few years can be managed by cleverly designed transitional periods. we must help the border regions to take advantage of their new opportunities and to meet the new challenges. the commission is already working on proposals to this end. there will be no environmental dumping and no social dumping because the new members will have to adopt our standards. the enlargement is good for the environment. enlargement is also good for the level of social security in europe. we also have the same standards and the same benefits of european cooperation in border security and crime prevention. here too, enlargement will make things better, not worse. the cost of enlargement is fixed in the present financial perspective. you'll find yourself in the household. more than is budgeted cannot and will not be spent. let me sum up the message to the citizens of europe. enlargement offers us a unique opportunity to change the course of european history and to secure peace for the peoples of europe, to preserve stability and to open up new and great life opportunities for all. the enlargement is not an adventure. it is prepared as thoroughly as possible. it would be an adventure to abandon the project or postpone it indefinitely. we have a window of opportunity. it's open now. it won't stay open forever. there are risks. no one can avoid that. but we must not let that deter us from our resolve. it would be a much greater risk not to do what needs to be done! (applause from the centre)
mr president, president-in-office, commissioner, i am extremely pleased both with the tone of this debate today and with the level of consensus that is emerging among the key groups in the house, a consensus which my group subscribes to. the challenge of reunification, even more perhaps than that of enlargement is so great that it suffuses and surrounds every debate in this house: this is why there is such depth and intensity to all our debates, including this morning's debate on our own need to prepare for this extraordinary challenge. to create a common space of freedom, security and of values by free choice is something without a parallel among democracies in all of our history. it is a matter of the utmost importance and yet has curiously attracted very little public attention. when we look at eurobarometer statistics, we see that 60% or so of those who expressed a view say enlargement is not a priority, while only 27% say it is a priority. moreover, the statistics are getting worse, not better. in the largest state of the union, the federal republic of germany, only 20% say enlargement is a priority. we as politicians must take possession of this debate and not simply leave it to the bureaucracies, for though it is indispensable to discuss the details of the acquis, this is not sufficient to engage the public. we need a dialogue of politicians and therefore we need to avail ourselves of all the information possibilities that commissioner verheugen spoke of in his very reflective and elegant speech today. we need, as mr hänsch has said, to provide ourselves with information about the cost of non-enlargement, not simply in terms of finance and budget, but of security as well as, of course, socio-economic measures. it was franklin delano roosevelt, the american president, who once said that we have nothing to fear but fear itself. much of our debate about fears about enlargement is based on exaggeration, but in order to confront the populists who exaggerate, we must connect with popular politics through reflection. we need the commission's help here, because even though we have the will, we sometimes lack the ammunition. in respect of the principles involved, we of course support differentiation; of course, we support the principle of equality that there should not be a europe of different classes of citizenship. my group has 23 visiting mps, our "virtual meps", sitting in the gallery now: i welcome all of you to this debate in the house. finally, the critical thing now is to move to substantive negotiation, and we are making that important step. the critical thing is not to allow frustration to grow among the candidates – that they are always in the anteroom but never quite in the chamber. that is what we must accomplish in this debate, that is the result we must pursue. people joke that after the collapse of the berlin wall enlargement is always just five years away. we must show they are wrong. let us take the first step in that direction during this mandate.
mr president, i want to focus my remarks on the orphanages in romania. i believe that the romanian government has problems in estimating the number of orphans who are presently in the orphanage system in that country. in correspondence received by the european commission delegation office in bucharest from the romanian government it is estimated that there are between 100 000 and 140 000 orphans in romania at present. the brutality of the ceausescu regime ensured that so many children were orphaned in romania since the late 60s. there are up to 100 000 people employed within the romanian orphanages themselves. this is a complicating factor when one addresses this issue. i visited romania at the end of july to see at first hand the workings of one of the largest orphanages which is in northern romania. it is clear to me that the institutional, ordered regime which operates in that country results in many long-term health consequences for orphaned children. it was also clear that the staff who work in romanian orphanages need further child care opportunities. many of the orphanages in romania are old, antiquated buildings. new, modern facilities such as those built by the romanian challenge appeal are going to be needed if the quality of life for orphaned children is going to be improved. i visited two of these houses that i can recommend to anyone. i saw at first hand what benefit these homes – and they are homes – are bringing to the orphaned children. it is imperative that the accession negotiations between romania and the union detail exactly what the romanian government needs to do to improve the standard and quality of orphanages in that country. it is not sufficient to make commitments. they must act. i had the opportunity of meeting with non-governmental organisations in romania which are working with the orphanages. i publicly commend the work of these and, in particular, the romanian challenge appeal based in birmingham and others from ireland supported by a number of people including daniel o'donnell, the irish entertainer, who has pledged gbp 1 million and has raised that in ireland and other parts of the world. it is people like this who have done so much. we must ensure that romania is not allowed into the european union unless it addresses this issue.

circumstances beyond their control. central to this very wide debate is the white paper on food safety which will for the first time propose an ethical framework putting europe and its member states at the forefront of world food safety. it has been brought about by the clear and unequivocal concerns of 370 million consumers in the european union on the safety of the european food chain. member detected. the union must also, by means of directives, establish a legal basis to harmonise the transmission of information on feed controls and food safety. i should like to comment briefly on the possibility of authorising the use of raw materials for animal food in excess of the maximum limits laid down. these directives are part of the most radical shake-up of the community's food safety hygiene rules for at least 25 years. the new regulations will merge, harmonise and simplify very detailed and complex hygiene requirements which were previously scattered over numerous directives. we in writing. - in april 1999, the commission proposed a major reform of the community competition system as regards the application of article 81 (prohibiting agreements between undertakings) and article 82 (prohibiting abuse of a dominant position). the rapporteur welcomes the commission's white paper proposals on this subject, but has concerns about the possible implications of the reform, the main aim of which is to decentralise the application of the competition rules. it seems to the commission that the abolition of the notification and authorisation systems and the strengthening of the role of national legal systems in disputes concerning community competition law will lead to a renationalisation of this policy. while we do not agree with all aspects of eu competition policy, it is nevertheless a policy that has paid off for 43 years and has helped to prevent abuse of a dominant position by companies based in the european union. i would not want us to be in a situation where the primacy of community law in the application of the union's competition rules could be threatened in any way. the uniform application of european competition law throughout the eu must be a sovereign principle. i therefore welcome the rapporteur's call on the european commission to provide additional guarantees as regards the application of competition law in the member states. these guarantees will include, in particular, the closer involvement of the assembly in the development and implementation of competition policy and in the monitoring of the quality of competition legislation in the various eu member states and the candidate countries. clear mechanisms for allocating cases between national authorities and the european commission should be provided for in order to avoid applicants being able to choose the legal system that would be most advantageous to them. the decentralised application of community law can only be successful if clear commitments are made to ensure that it is applied uniformly throughout the european union.
in writing. -en) state aid has traditionally been used by member states as an instrument of industrial and social policy. despite the measures taken to promote greater deregulation within the european union, state aid still has a constructive role to play in social and economic development. i am a member of this house for the connaught/ulster constituency. the whole of this region was classified as an objective 1 area for the seven-year period from 2000 to 2006. when a region is classified as objective 1, firms setting up there are entitled to a higher percentage of state aid than in a non-objective 1 region. in practice, new enterprises established in the objective 1 region in ireland are entitled to 40% of state subsidies for fixed investment. a supplementary agreement for 15% of fixed investment costs is also possible for small and medium-sized enterprises. for the purposes of this state subsidy, any company with fewer than 250 employees is considered to be a small or medium-sized enterprise. the possibility of granting such state subsidies to enterprises established in the objective 1 region is of obvious economic and social interest. this measure ensures that further population movements from rural to urban areas are stopped. it also provides for greater inward investment in the region and is an effort to increase employment opportunities for young people living there. i welcome the recent statement that aid ireland is radically changing its methods to attract more jobs to the objective 1 area of ireland. aid ireland has refocused its activity to attract half of all greenfield projects jobs to the objective 1 region over the next three years. this policy change is beginning to take effect and with time, and through the implementation of government infrastructure programmes as described in the national development plan for the period 2000 to 2006, the ida may be confident in its ability to achieve its objectives in terms of new projects for the objective 1 region. too many regions are currently disadvantaged by their non-competitive infrastructure and the lack of services to support entrepreneurship, which are essential to i welcome the opportunity to make a brief contribution to this important debate and to dwell on the importance of the agricultural budget and its relevance not just to farmers but to society in general. those with an interest in developing a sustainable agricultural sector will be aware that the central thrust of recent reform as agreed in berlin is a continuing move from commodity support to direct income support for farmers. so it is both a social and an economic measure and it is designed to bring prices into line with world trade, while at the same time attempting to retain the european model based on family farming. a point not always made is that this sizeable budget provision is not just confined to support for farmers but is also a factor in the provision of cheap food for european citizens. in some areas of agricultural activity, such as bee production, eu support represents the profit margin which in effect means consumers enjoy a quality food product at production costs and regretfully, as demonstrated in recent years, sometimes below the cost of production. in berlin last year it was agreed that the budgetary provision for agriculture for the period 2000-2006 will be over £506 billion, that the 15 member states of the european union will continue to contribute 1.3% of gross national product to the overall annual budget – which of course includes measures to finance the proposed enlargement of the european union, a proposal which i and my other colleagues fully support. we fully support the concept of enlargement and the provision of sufficient resources to make the applicant states’ transition to membership meaningful. despite what has been agreed, i sometimes wonder if it is possible to achieve this objective within existing budget constraints without undermining the agricultural budget necessary for a sustainable rural sector in existing member states. in this regard i believe that we have to act as politicians; we cannot expect to be magicians in a situation where there may not be sufficient funds to develop the economies of the countries of central and eastern europe along the lines that the existing member states would want to see them developed.

mr president, while the european food chain is one of the safest in the world, poor practice, and indeed recent scandals, have set an agenda to which our commissioner, mr byrne, has responded
